What the Suffragists Mean to do.—Ths Government is nnderstood to be rather concerned over the growing agitation nr tha political enfranchisement of women. They were inclined at first to regard the Forward Suffrarft Movement as a matter that need not be taken seriously. It is a thorny subject, but there is no doubt on which side rests the weight of opinion amongst working women, and this is an influence which the Government would rather have friendly than hostile to them. Meanwhile the women agitators are appealing for funds for en organised movement and declare that they have by no mesns done with Mr. Asquith, and other members of the Cabinet whom they regard as enemies to their cause. Demonstrations are to be con- tinued not only in London but in Manchester, Leeds, Glasgow, York, Huddersfield, and many other of the leading cities and towns in the North.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Improper use of Private Latters.—The reckless publication of private letters by biographers and others has often been deprecated, and it shocked Charles Dickens so much, that he destroyed every private letter he had received from any notable person. One is reminded of this by an incident whine took place last week, when a letter was sold at auction which had been addressed by the late Queen Victoria to her son, the present Duke of Connaught. There is no suggestion that anybody obtained possession of the document by improper means, but one would have thought that the original letter at any rate would not have been per- mitted to come into the market. The Heir to Hawarden.—The Hawarclen estates, to which Mr. William C. Gladstone has just succeeded on his coming of age, came to the late Mr. W. E. Gladstone through his wife. The historic flawArdeti Castle was dismantled during the Civil War and is only a ruin, but the modern building is famous is the home where the great statesman lived and died. Mr. Morley has told the story how the estates which extend to over 700 wres were rescued from financial confusion by the late Air. W. E. Gladstone. and they now bring in about £13,000 a year chiefly from mining royalties. The new owner is a son of the late Mr. W. IL Gladstone, and a grandson of the statesman. He inherits the Liberal traditions of the family, and intends to follow a political career. St. Swithin's tradition.—ln Prance, the day of the festival of St. Gervais (June 19) is marked by a similar superstition as we attach to the 15th July, viz., that if it rains on St.' Swithin's day,there will be rain for forty days after. In neither country do the records bear out these traditions. Moreover, rain seldom falls all over the country at the same time. It is no uncommon thing in the summer for it to rain on one side of the Toad and not on the other, and when, on this particular day it rains in one country, and not in the next, there are two opposite interpretations of the legend, and .experience shows that one is just as likely to be right As the other. At the same time there is no doubt some truth in the old belief that the first fortnight in July will show what the summer is to be. If that is so, the omens this year are distinctly favourable. Some people have hurried on their summer holidays for fear that as we have had so much fine weather a wet season lies ahead, but this is not according to the old belief that fine weather over the half year means a fine summer.

Useful Serge.—For the river, linen and muslin have I somewhat ousted serge, but the latter fabric promises to reign supreme at Cowes. White and cream will lead, but our old friend navy blue, trimmed with white, or red, will be much in evidence. Her Majesty Queen Alexandra has a great affection for navy blue serge, touched with white, so also has the Princes Royal, but the Princess of Wales and Princess Victoria invariably prefer all white, or cream serge toilettes. Undoubtedly 1 for yachting serge scores, especially the home-made 1 navy serge, which resists with true John Bull sturdiness 1 all attempts to deprive it of its original possessions of utility and cornliness of texture. Travelling Hats and Veils.—The long gauze veil floating behind, appears to be greatly beloved by vast numbers of women tourists—foreigners for the most part—this summer, but as, worn in this particular way it is neither useful or ornamental, it is better dispersed with. The only possible excuse for such an accessory is when it is required for securinv.headgear and keeping turbulent locks tidy. Veils for use when travelling, should be entirely free from floating effects,and absolute neatness should be the order of the day. When starting upon a long railway journey the wise woman too will not omit to slipinto into her roll of wraps and rugs, a soft tweed cap. The comfort of such an addition to one's luggage is beyond mere words, moreover the damage usually caused to ahat or toque by the showers of cinders and clouds of dust, which inevitably accom- ?zany the traveller who loves fresh air,and open windows is thus averted. Dust Wraps.—The most extraordinary bargains in dust coats are being offered at the sales, and those among us who have to be contented with one and only, would be well advised to select it built of silk tussore, in some light shade of grey or fawn. The cut should be of the simplest and no fixed trimmings sliould be thought of. The garment should be stitched only, and an adjustable collar and undersleeves of lace provided so that in a few moments, the coat could be turned into a dressy affair, for a garden party, race meetings, or any other outdoor festivity. When soiled, it could be 4" dipped" either dark grey, brown, or blue, all of which colours the material will take splendidly. Embroidered Robes.—Japanese embroidered dresses are coming rapidly to the ,ront, and such an investment bids fair to prove a desirable one. The embroidery is beautifully done, the material itself is good and durable, and the effect produced is charming. At the garden party given by the Prince and Princess of Wales, at Marlborough House, last week, one beautiful woman appeared in a gown of this description, the fabric being crêpe of an apple pink hue, embroidered with white acacia blossom. A blonde girl "Ms wearing a honey colored crepe frock,upon which black and. white butter:

flies were gaily disporting themselves, while a decided brunette had donned a pearl-grey crepe gown, embroidered with storks. These Japanese embroidered robes will wear and clean splendidly, and are really less expensive than many ordinary gowns. Hosiery and Footwear.—Coloured hosiery and foot- wear are being worn with summer frocks, and the boot and shoe manufacturers, who have long been trying to get one to appreciate the beauty of coloured leather. seem at length to have success within their grasp. For wear with white toilettes, pale grey shoes and hosiery are the vogue, and present a very neat and smart appearance, bright colours, and any note of contrasting footwear being avoided by the best dressed women. DRESS-FASHION-HOME.

A FRENTR IDEA.

Now that all thoughts are turned towards sim- plicity in table decoration, a scheme s3en lately at the house of a French lady may not appeal on account of its elaboration; but the elaboration is more in the description than in the reality. which was quite effective, quaint, and taste:Mi. Should any reader attempt thi; decoration, sl:.• must remember that its success depends entirely on airiness and lightnass of arrangement. Here is the idea; a series of gilded amboo arclien, running from end to end of the table, each arc'n decorated with orchids, maidenhair fern. and Louis Seize bows of mauve ribbon. Though the erection was kept low enough not to interfere with the comfort of the guests, the little golden arches bore small bunches of grapes, baskets of strawberries, and fancy cases of bon-bons, thus adding utility to beauty. A small bouquet, tied with a mauve ribbon bow, was placed at each guest's place, the trail of ribbon and fern • from one bouquet touching on to the nest, until they finished at each corner in a nest of orchids and II lattice work of ribbons.

LADIES' TAILORING.

It is indicative of the strong hold ladies' tailors have secured upon their clients to find the steady demand still devolving upon them. Usu- ally at this period of the year, the Tailor and Cutter points out, the essential characteristic of the tailor's gown appears a little inappropriate. Summery attire is more in keeping with tropical conditions than a cloth robe, but the excellent qualities in perfections of fit and design of the latter is no doubt responsible for the creation of many tailors' unconventionalities now going through. Flannel costumes are in good request, and linen gowns are to be seen in some tailors' establishments.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
BLACK GOWN STYLE.

The possibilities of the black gown are on such a grand and extensive scale that its variations are easily adapted to the young, the middle- aged, and the elderly, and although its primary fabric should be of fine quality, good colour, and of fairly substantial texture, writes a gossiper in the Lady, I would entreat the intending wearer carefully to consider her own figure before she decides upon the shape and make. For a very ample figure, a slight pouch in front takes off a good deal from the size, and a straight belt is quite unsuitable, but the rucked belt, which is rather wide at the back and narrows off in front, is delightful with a pouched vest or blouse, or even where cascades of lace meander down the front and droop over the skirt.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
DAINTY NECLIGE.

In our sketch is. depicted a dainty neglig6, which could be carried out very inexpensively ha muslin or mercerised lawn in . white or any of the pretty pale colours. On the shoulders the gown is arranged in a deep yoke, which is con- cealed beneath the cape or collar, which con- sists of broderie anglaise, finished with two full frills edged,. with Valenciennes lace. Over each shoulder there are two long strips of embroidery, which meet in a point at the waist behind, and are continued down to the hem in front, strips of embroidery outlining the noel:. The material is gathered very closely, and is drawn in at the

waist by a ribbon belt, which is passed beneath the embroidery and tied loosely in front, the 'hem being finished with two wide tucks. The sleeves consist of two very full frills, each edged with Valenciennes lace.

BEAUTY PARASOLS.

Parasols are playing a very important part this summer. There are the little ones, with folding handles and dangling fringes, and there are the embroidered muslin "shades," with linings of palest mousseline de soie or of pleated gauze. The real " beauty parasol " f the season, how- ever. is made of pearl-grey éhiffon and lined with shell-pink crepe de chine. ,Ono of these exquisite sunshades intended for Goodwood has the stick of polished ebony, with a handle of filigree silver inset with baroque pearls. The sunshade is not very large, but it has several pleated frills of the two delicate colours, and it casts a most flatter- ing shade over the face. Now that pale grey shoes and gloves are so much worn with white gowns. a' parasol would be ideal, but the pink must be of the faintest and softest shade, and likewise the pearl-grey chiffon. Some people cherish an idea that red parasols are becoming; certainly they are cheerful, but unless to an exceedinily pale skin they are by no means flat- tering. On a hot day they make the average complexion look as though it were on fire.

TO-DAY'S RECIPE.

STUFFED CABBAGE.—Take a fresh cabbage and remove the larger leaves carefully, washing them well in salted water. Have ready some boiling water, well salted; throw in the cabbage leaves, and cook for five minutes. Let them remain in the water until they are soft, and then cut away the hard outer stalk. Take a breakfastcupful o? lentils which have been soaking for twelve hours and boiled for half.an-hour, and the same quantity of cooked rice. Max these together, add a teaspoonful of chopped mushrooms, double the quantity of minced shallot, a IFttle seasoning of salt and pepper, and a squeeze of lemon-juice. Fut a little of the mixture into the centre of isach cabbage leaf, roll up—folding the edges over neatly—and place the stuffed leaves in rows Ar, tne nottom or a erewpam rour in summenz water—well 'seasoned—to cover, them, and Sim- mer for an hour. Dish them c4refully so as not to break the rolls, and serve with a goad tomato

A SON OF EMPIRE.

BY MORLEY ROBERTS, Author of "The Western Avernus," " Strong Melt and True," "The Earth Mother," "Red Earth," "The Great Jester,",

===== 0002601_19060721_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XX.

The dread of things Amelia would say had ruined what at one time had promised to be a fine career. She sent him into Parliament when he did not want to go; she dragged him out when he wished to stay; she had a headache and a distinct pain when anything displeased her even in the first years of their marriage. And now to tell her this news about Madge, when he knew she had set her heart on the girl's marrying young Maitland, was a perfectly appalling task. _

Madge watched his wrinkled brow, and through her tears saw every motion of his mind. "And oh, dad, you won't tell mother yet?" "No," said her father, "I won't—yet." "And not till I ask you, please." He looked at her doubtfully. "Yon told me this in confidence, didn't you?' "Of course," said Madge. "Then I suppose I musn't, though I ought to," said Mr. Gretton, with a sigh of relief. "And don't speak to Sir Dan about—him," said Madge, with downcast eyes, "because Sir Dan hates him."

"Hates him? Why's that ? " "Well—he does," said Madge. "It was Sir Dan who made it so hot for him in India." "So hot for him in India, eh ? " mused her father. "Confound it Is Sir Dan responsible for the climate ?" Madge laughed and pretended it was a huge joke. "But you know what I mean." And she got up from the floor and sat on his knee, and with her arm about his neck said he was a darling, and the best father in the entire universe. And she wondered if he would ever know about that telegram, and if so what he would think. "Where does this scoundrel of yours live, now ? " asked Mr. Gretton presently. "I—believe he's staying at 80, Duke-street, S. James's, dad."

Mr. Oretton sighed. "Well, if you believe it. I daresay he may be heard of there, Madge. Good-night, dear." She clung round his neck. "Am I so horrid ? "No, my darling." • am." She kissed him again and again, and left him sitting before the fire in strange desolation. For years she had been home to him—home, pleasure, pride, and delight. And now the golden dragon, India, might swallow her up for many long years, as it had done his boy. Amelia, untenmered by her children, offered a rank prospect; she would be more intolerable than ever. But if she was he would go out to India himself. The thought of freedom encouraged him. He rose from his chair in better heart, and, taking a drink of whisky, con- founded Amelia and her works. He felt far more friendly to this interloping Blundell, who offered him a chance of liberty, and sitting down wrote him a letter-. He posted it himself. He went to his club early next morning, and at eleven Blundell's card was brought in to him. The two men looked into each other's eyes and shook hands. At first Gretton felt that Blundell was far too old for Madge; he noticed the touch of grey above his ears. And yet he was a very fine fellow. "I hope you don't feel very hostile to me, sir ?" said Blundell, frankly, after the first uneasy moment. "You must know I do, Captain Blundell," answered Gretton, rather sadly. He looked older than his years. "I can understand it."

"She is my only daughter." Richard thanked him inwardly for having one. VO did not require two. "I know your son, Mr. Gretton," he said, irrelevantly; "and he's a very fine young fellow. Perhaps you know we were together with the Karak column?" "Of course I know," said the father, proudly, "and I'm glad to meet you on that account. I only learnt yesterday that you were in town. And —if it hadn't been for difficulties I should have asked you to dine with us. But I didn't know you knew Margaret." Richard looked at him straight. "Our first meeting was accidental and rather in- formal, sir." "So I understand," said Gretton, a little drily. "Mrs. Gretton distinctly disapproves of it. And I am not prepared to say that I approved of it myself." "Oh, sir, it was very natural," cried Richard. "The devil's in nature!" growled Gretton. "And I understand you want to marry her?" "Certainly, 'said Richard. "Did she not say so ? " "Of course she said so. But it's hardly a ques- • tion of what she said. This is a serious matter." "I think she is serious, too, sir," cried Richard, warmly. He looked much younger, and Mr. Gretton noted it. "How old are you?" he asked, abruptly. " Thirty-eight. " "And she's nineteen." f. "Nearly twenty, sir." P.. "There's a great disparity." r"Not too great, Mr. Gretton. I'm really a very young man. And now I have very good prospects." "Inn," cried Gretton; "and so has she. Do you know she will have money, and a good deal of it, on the death of her soother? A remote thing, of course, for Mrs. Gretton, I'm happy to say, is so strong that all the doctors in London can't kill her." Richard looked at him with a faint glint in his eye. "I'm happy to hear both statements, Mr. Gretton. Money is an excellent thing, an in. dispensable thing. I don't undervalue it." "And may I ask what 'you have of your own? Most men- in the Indian Army leave their English regiments because of money." Richard nodded. "I was in the Hussars, and could have managed to stay there on that account." "Then why did you lea-ve your regiment ?" It was rather an awkward question, but Richard answered it. "I was a young fool of twenty, and I got the General Officer commanding down on me." "What about 2" asked Gretton, ruthlessly. And to his own astonishment and to Gretton's he actually blushed. "It was about a—lady, Sir." "That's bad," grunted Gretton. "I deeply regret it," said Richard; "but it was eighteen years ago, and I have learnt sense since then. You would not think the folly of a boy, which was bitterlst pond for, any imnediment ?"

===== 0002601_19060721_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER XXI.

But the worst was not over; perhaps it was only beginning. If Madge had been just a little more suspicious or a little less wrapped up in herself and Richard, that smile of her mother's at dinner-time might have warned her of pits and coining disasters. Mrs. Gretton said she was not better, but more able to bear it. and the reason was that she had again heard from Sir Dan. What she learnt from the Adjutant-General's letter Richard 'Blundell knew when he left Mr. Gretton and went to his own club. For he found there a letter from.the Intelligence Department, asking him to call on Major-General Turner, the chief of the branch. "Now, what the deuce does he want with me ?" asked Richard, with a frown. "Does he want to know anything about Gulistan and Chitral and the passes of it? Or would he like my opinion of the shooting at Dum-Dum ? or is he going to offer me some confounded appointment in the purlieus of Fall Mall " He smoked two perique cigarettes. • "I'll go round now, he cried presently ;ansi clapping his hat on, he stalked through St. James's- square. He was just a little apprehensive of un- welcome gifts. For things had gone wondrous well for him with Madge's father. The old chap—who was' by no means an old chap at all—was delightful, and would make a paragon of a father-in-law. And Richard was eager -Co try him in that character. Every day saw him deeper in love with ,Madge. She was the poden of Cashmere to his sultry, sun- burnt India; nightingales sang in her kroves ; sire was a strange, sweet Paradise to one who ha, wandered for long, unhappy year si by the confines of the Pit. , Bless her a thousand times for wl;r,; she had done, for what she was, and for what sh. "'Quid

-

MajoC-General 'Turner was in the office, and would see Captain Blundell at once. „- . .

"Oonfound him ! What's the answer to the puzzle?" thought Richard, and he was before the answerer the next minute. - He found him to his for Turner was a decent little fellow, though far from being a typical soldier, according to popular notions, or even according to notions popular in the Service. "I heard of your being in town only a couple of days ago," said Turner, "and when I heard of it, the thought occurred to inc that you were the very man we wanted." _ _

Richard acknowledged the compliment gloomily. "One so often has to work with the best at hand that the best there is seems a rare piece of luck," smiled Turner.

"Confound it I What beastliness does he want me for now? "• thought Richard. "Anything I can do for you I shall be only too glad to do, sir," he replied, aloud. "I wanted a man with zeal, tact, discretion, all of which, lam glad to say, can be had pretty easily," said Turner; "but I wanted also a man who knows the Soudan."

"0 LOrd ! " cried Blundell, internally. "Islow I'm in for it!"

"Your previous experience there, when you went through from Somaliland by way of Hamar, will be of the greatest assistance to you and to us. And your knowledge of the languages, which is known to be extraordinary, fits you to be the head of a confidential expedition we are sending down through the Hinterland of Abyssinia." „—. . .

"Thank you, sir," said Richard, sombrely. .Te saw the blazing desert aanin, and the murderous sun glaring through a redsand-storm. The groan- ing camels bared their teeth and grinned unhappily; men died and withered to mummies. Madge's oasis was a fond, foolish mirage. And yet, after his banishment from activity, to spring to the front again, and to lead an expedition was much—very much.. . _

"I understand you have six months' leave from Tudia.." gala Turner, Pleasantly, "and. of course. it shall be extended to any amount. We expect you will be away a year; if you are lucky, it may be less." Richard nodded. His mind worked furiously; he was on the horns of a dilemma. To take this might be to lose Madge; to refuse might ruin him again in the Service. He could not take it. He must take it.

But Turner never imagined that he would not. He began a lecture on the politics of the expedition. Richard heard him as if he was talking through a speaking-tube. The pretentions of the Abyssinians; the ambition of the French to come in from the French Congo; Egypt and the Soudan; Darfour and British Equatorial Africa; a north and south Transcontinental telegraph; the British Govern- ment, and schemes originating in Capetown, buzzed in his ears, and were piped at him thinly. For little Turner was an Imperialist of the first water, and, sitting at his desk, translated many ideas into action.

"We are anxious that this should be carried through without noise, ostentation, or newspaper paragraphs," said Turner at length, "and you will especially direct your attention to making the expedition a silent and bloodless one. The less that is heard about it the better. You will get no public kudos for it, Captain Blundell. But if you succeed, as I know you will, you will have me at your back in obtaining any appointment that your service entitles you to apply for. When can you start?" "Whenever you like, sir," said Richard, dreamily. "In a week from now, then," cried Turner. —You will find all preparations made for you on your arrival at Zeila. I will let you have your final instructions in three days. Call here for them. I am extremely glad to haNe seen you, Captain Blundell. You did very well, I know, in Gulistan, but if you carry this through you will find it even more to your credit." "I am always ready —ao my duty, air," said Richard, grimly. And going outside, he cursed the entire Intelligence Department, from Major-General Turner at the top down to the meanest copyist at ten- pence an hour. What a slave a man was ! Here he was politely borrowed from the India Office, as though he was a second division clerk or a telegraph operator, and it was practically impossible to refuse to be lent. Again he saw the confounded plains of the lower Soudan, and between far wells skeletons lay half buried in the drifting sand. Dust devils, the black and red magicians of the desert, stalked hissing over a fiery land. His mouth was dry; Ile hit on dust.

Be wrote to Madge when he got back to his rooms. And as he wrote with strange and melan- choly passion, his heart bled for her more than for himself.. For in him disaster on disappoint- ment and disappointment on disaster had bred something akin to Oriental fatalism. For as fatalism is the more peculiar heritage of the race which has been beaten a thousand times, it is bestowed on the man who has failed. He felt be could endure anything now; even if Madge were taken from him the despair would lead to no out- burst of repining. For what is written is written, and God is Great

But for this sweet child, now no more a child, but in the first glory of her womanhood and her first passion, he knew his going would be like the bitter- ness of death. Perhaps she would not recognise the necessity for it ; she might imagine for one moment of anguish that he desired to go ; that he did not love her; that his love Ead died, or had never existed. And even if she saw that it was impossible.for him to refuse, she mould imagine, withjiwift fertility of dark forebodings, no return out of the bitter desert. A year of absence meant a year of suspense. And yet, would she remember? She had remembered when he had forgotten, and by remembering had created him anew. Now, out of her efforts for him came this period of trial ; the gods had granted her petition with ironic laughter.' He wrote her a long letter, and told her his heart, his hopes, his fears. For many, many years he had not broken down the barriers between him- self and any human soul, but now at last they were swept away in flood; he could endure no narrow limits ;his words were instinctive, alive ; they ached with passion; were musical and strange. This was their marriage; the infinite passion of this dark, restrained man, unrestrained, overwhelmed her like Love itself.

For when Madge read his words in the morning she knew how utterly he was hers at last, and not even the anguish of imminent parting could' destroy the deep content with which she made peace with her own soul. She had given herself away without being asked; she had bestowed her love at sight, and had wrought for a return. Now his love was hers without any reservation ; she saw him writing ; beheld his stern, set face grow 'mobile under the influence of rare emotion. She had conquered her love, had won her kingdom; living or dead 'she was his for ever; he was hers though his bones Whitened ,on the Nubian desert, though they met no more tinder the canopy of Heaven.

SCHOOLBOY KILLED ON THE RAILWAY.

William Thompson, 11, a Great Harwood schoolboy' was examining a bird trap set between the railway metals whilst shunting operations were going on, when be was caught by a wagon and terribly mutilated, death being instantaineous.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES,

Bold-street Wesleyan Church

At this Church on Sunday morning the Rev. J. Manning preached on "Life's difficulties and how to meet them." At the evening service the preacher was the Rev. Frederick R. Bell, of Widnes.

J. it Seddon, M.P., at Penketh. Mr. 3. A. Seddon, M.P., was present at the open meeting of the Penketh Wesleyan Men's Bible Class on Sunday, and delivered-a lecture in the Wesleyan Church entitled " Christianity v. Atheism." Mr. Charles E. Parker, the president, presided over a large attendance. Miss L. Plinston, of Warrington, sang two solos, and Mr. G. W. Weaver officiated at the orga.n. The lecturer dealt with his subject in an able manner, and contended that everyone was bound to admit there was a Supreme Being. He trusted that everyone present would endeavour to carry out Christ's teachings.

Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. The Rev. S. Pitt preached at this church on Sunday morning and made an appeal on behalf of the West Indian Distress Fund. He described at some length the history of the mission and the reasons for the present crisis which were: the revolution in trade consequent upon the failure of the sugar industry, hurricane, earthquakes and Volanic disturbances which had brought the mission to the verge of bankruptcy. In the evening the pulpit was occupied by the Rev. J. Manning who preached on "The character of Samson." Mr. Pearce effectively sang the solo "Arm, arm ye brave " from " Judas Maccabaeus."

Latchford Baptist Church. There were large congregations at the Latchford Baptist Church on Sunday when the preacher was the Rev. J. F. Matthews. In the morning his subject was "a visionless people" and in the evening "The inwardness of fear." Preaching on the latter subject he said we ought to fear, not so much from the dread of future punishment, but from horror at the enormity of the results arising from our wrong doing and evil. We feared to wound where we loved—where we loved udeeplypon thwe e iwheerret iaelswwayhsi which castinglove acbroetai ttetdo avoid betweeni nl nwgo- souls—a ud the same fear should operate between our- selves and God. We should fear to wound Him because we loved Him deeply. Church Sunday School Association. The quarterly meeting of the Warrington Church Sunday School Association was held, at Padgate, on Saturday. The afternoon was spent at a garden party in the vicarage grounds, which were looking their best and, as the weather was highly favourable for out-door amusements, the time was passed most agreeably, At 5.30, by which time nearly 100 had assembled, tea was partaken of in the National School. The tables were daintily set out, and much praise was bestowed upon Mrs. Grounds, who was responsible for the arrange- ments. -At 7.15 a service was held in Christ Church, and was largely attended. The service was read by the Rev. W. Pollock-Hill, the preacher being Canon Spooner, Archdeacon of Warrington, who deliverecian address on the work of the Sunday school teacher the importance of which he said weuld be much increased if the Education Bill were passed. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. There WAS a fair attendance at the cleating on Sun when the Hon. Secretary (Mr. B. Farrar) presid spoke upon " Pleasing memories." He said that A remember things they desired to forget was ate.r good for them as way marks, and reminders of thel, great weakness, and often drew men to God their grea', Helper. But the memories that were pleasant—gne" living, faithful service, devotion to duty—came to them as a great reward now and filled them with prospective joy for time and eternity.—Mr. F. Fooks, of Manchester, delivered an address upon "Fighters "—not in ft physical sense, but from an intellectual, moral' MR" spiritual point of view. Prayer was the great leier which made for strength and made them mighty against sin. Mr. W. H. Payton, Mils. Bite., presided at the organ. Mr. Lewis took part in the meeting. £1 Ils Scl was received for the organ fund, the Secretary asking the brethren to take out subscription cards for their wives and friends. A pleasing feature of the meeting was the very hearty sin ging of the hymns.

Re-Opening Services at St. Paul's. The re-opening services in connection with St. Paul's Church were continued on Sunday, when there were large congregations, the preacher in the morning being the Vicar. In the afternoon a children's service was held, followed by a united Bible class service, the preachers being the Rev. H. Bowen, curate, and the Rev. John Colon, a former curate. At the evening service the Vicar again preached,alluding to the wonder- ful transformation which bad been effected in the appearance of the Churcb, and shelved what important lessons might be derived in one's daily life from the cleansing and renovation. He hoped their minds and hearts would be renovated and that their bodies would become temples of the Holy. Ghost. He paid a high compliment to Mr. John Hesketh, the decorator, in making what they thought it at one time was impossible such a beautiful church. An anthem was very creditably rendered by the choir and precessional and recessional hymns were sung. Collections were made in aid of the renovation fund.

OPEN-AIR CAMPAIGN.

The open-air campaign under the auspices of the Warrington Free Church Council is being continued with enthusiasm. Since the combined meetinr, on the fair ground the various churches have formed special groups which operate in the various districts. On Sun- day evening last the Latchford group held a meeting in Thelwall-lane; Stockton Heath people met opposite the Mulbery Tree Hotel, and the Bewsey-road group in Napier-street. All the meetings were well attended and were highly successful. The Rev. J. F. Matthews was desirous that during the winter months the council should arrange for services to be held in the common lodging houses of the town as by this means he is con- vinced that they will be able to reach the class whom it is most desired to influence—Last (Thursday) evening another of the series of open-air services was held in Penketh.

£20,000 COMPENSATION FOR MR. CYRIL MAUDE Speaking to the proprietors of the South-Eastern Railway on Tuesday, Mr. Cosmo Bonsor announced that the company had paid £20,000 as compensation to Mr. Cyril Maude in connection with the accident to the Avenue Theatre when the roof of Charing Cross Rail- way Station collapsed.

COUNTY EDUCATION COMMITTEES.

LANCASHIRE. THE TRAINING OF TEACHERS.

Sir Henry Hibbert, at the Lancashire Education Committee meeting at Preston on Monday, said the time bad arrived for immediate action to provide addi- tional accommodation in the county for the training of of teachers. Training college accommodation was undoubtedly deficient for undenominational teaching, although there was plenty for denominational. It would be necessary in the near future to provide undenominational colleges so that they could have a better class of teachers. At present certificated teachers in Lancashire only numbered 43 per cent., compared with 56 per cent. of the whole country. The committee were trying by a system of municipal secondary sohools to induce a better class of girls to go in for the teach- ing profession, and they therefore anticipated that, seeing teachers were being taken from a better class, there would be a much larger number wishing to go into training colleges.____

_ CHESHIRE. • "THE HORRIBLE ASSOCIATION GAME."

-- A meeting of the Cheshire Education Committee was held at Crewe on Monday. The Director of Education in a report upon the more important changes in the new code of regulations for elementary schools, pointed out that provision was now made for suitable organised games for boys and girls during school hours, to form part of the school routine. He presumed the committee would be permitted to pay rents for playing fields. Colonel Thorpe said he would very much like to see Rugby football—a purely amateur game—encouraged In the schools in preference to the horrible Association game. Several members took exception to the committee taking playing fields for games, expressing the opinion also that the school honrs now were such that they could ill-spire a whole school afternoon every week for the purpose. The report was adopted.

ITAYDOCK

At a meeting of Haydock District Council last Thursday, the chief basinesi was the appointment of a medical officer of health in succession to tbe late Dr. Hayward. The contest lay between Drs. Dowling and ThOmpson, and the former secured the appointment by 6 votes to 5.

He (Mr. Plinstoo) happened to know some- thing of the lack of accomniodati, n in Warrington and at the present time a large /limber of children were debarred from attending school because of the fact that at the schools nearest to their homes there was no accommodation, and they could not he compelled to go to schools beyond a certain distance. Had the matter been gone into two years ago he believed there would I have been a great saving to the authority, and he was rather surprised that after such a long delay the com- mittee had at last come to this determination. He hoped the matter would be pressed forward. He was not willing to put all the blame on the sub-committee. He believed a great deal of the blame should be put on the shoulders of certain members of the Town Council who, for reasons best known to themselves, had played shuttlecock and paddle with a system that WAS for the benefit of the ratepayers, and especially of the children. Councillor ELLISON endorsed Mr. Plinston's remarks and asked for information as to when the Bolton school I would be proceeded with. Councillor Willow'. called attention to the fact that a letter had been received from the Town Clerk asking the committees to arrange as far as possible for the painting and decorating of public buildings to be done during the winter months. Why had the committee decided to take no action in the rnaater ?

The CHAIRMAN said that with regard to accommoda- tion he was not personally fond of iron buildings but he supposed possibly they were the only means to be adopted under the circumstances.

FAIRCLOUGH, replying on the discussion, said it was very much more convenient for painting to be done in summer when there was a long holiday, rather than in the winter months. The committee would do their best to proceed with new buildings for the accommoda- tion of the school children. As Nfr. Plinston said, it had been a matter of playing shuttlecock with regard to the Bolton school and it had barred the way to proceeding with a school in the We!Meld street neighbourhood. However, they thought it would big better to erect a temporary school so that it could be removed elsewhere as re- flaked rather than goon the same lines as were adopted in the case of the Bolton school and let it be four years before they could do anything at all. • The minutes were approved. This concluded the meeting and the hairman con- Fratulated the members on having established a record in regard to the brevity of the proceedings which had only occupied ten minutes.

WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE.

"A NEW SCHOOL EVERY TWO YEARS."

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Education Committee was held at the Town Hall on Monday even- ing under the presidency of Alderman Bennett. There were also present :—Councillor Fulton Smith (vice- chairman), Alderman Evans, Councillors Wilkinson, Wright, Ellison, Ball, Revs. Canon Morley Stevenson and John Yonge, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, H. Roberts, C.E. Parker, E. Pliuston, H. Kingdon, .C. S. Burgess, R. T. Fairclough, C. H. Unsworth, G. Walker, J. Moore Murray (secretary and direetor), and J. Fairhurst (treasurer).

===== 0002601_19060721_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====
EXCESS OF SCHOOL FEES.

It having been reported to the Finance Committee that the fees received at the Silver-street Wesleyan school would, by the end of the school year, probably be in excess of the amount: allowed by the Board of Education, arrangements which had received the con- currence of the managers for avoiding such excess were considered and approved. The minutes were approved. SCHOOL MANAGEMENT COMMITTEE. TEACHERS AND,PHYSICAL INSTRUCTION. EVENING SCHOOL ARRANGEMENTS.

At a meeting of the School Management Sub-com- mittee the half-yearly reports of the head teachers upon the assistant teachers in theschools in the borough were received through the managers,and it was resolved that the same be approved. A letter was submitted from the Honorary Secretary of the Warrington Branch of the National Union of Teachers, asking the committee to receive a deputation in reference to the half-yearly reports upon the assist- ant teachers, and it was decided to receive the deputa- tion at the September meeting of the committee. Reports from the Board of Education upon the War- rington British, Warrington Parochial, and St. Ann's Schee* were received and noted, together with the comments of the managers in the case of the first and last-named schools. Consideration of the report in the case of the War- rington Parochial School was deferred until the com- ments of the managers are received. A letter from H.M. Inspector—enclosing his notes on the teaching of English in some of the publio elemen- tary schools in the borough—was submitted, and it was resolved that a copy of the notes be forwarded to each head teacher. A report upon the examination in physical exercises recently conducted by Mr. T. Chesterton on behalf of the committee, was received. The Director was instructed to taped to the committee as to the con- tinuance of th arrangements made with the Gymnasium Committee, which expire on the Ist November next. A report by the Head Master of the Secondary school upon the results of the King's Scholarship examinations 1905 and 1906, was submitted and noted. A letter was submitted from the Town Clerk, asking, on behalf of the Town Hall, Parks, and B tths Com- mittee, for further particulars in reference to the committee's application for permission to appoint an instructor to teach the boys from the schools in the borough attending the baths in the art of swimming. It was resolved that a deputation, consisting of Coun- cillors Lord, Pemberton, Fulton Smith, Wilkinson, and Ithe Chairman of the Committee (the Rev. Canon Steven- son), wait upon the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Com- mittee in reference to the matter. The committee proceeded to the consideration of the arrangements for the eveninschools for the sessien 1906-7. In reference to tbe Central Technical Institute (hitherto known as the Technical Institute), it was decided that the teachers and salaries of the occasional staff should be as last season, any additions thereto, or alterations therein that may be found necessary to be submitted to the committee. NEW FIRE STATION OPENED. Messrs.Grosfield's Preparedness for Fire.

- - "In case of fire please gives fortnight's notice." Such was the request which a practical joker last week attached to the door of. Messrs. Crosfield's new fire station. Could the writer of the above note have witnessed the rapid turn-out of the brigade on Monday he would at once have withdrawn his instructions or limited the length of the warning to a few seconds. Within forty-live seconds from the time of the sound- ing of the alarm the first detachment of men with a hose cart were on their way to the seat of the supposed outbreak. Three seconds later the smaller of the two steam engines owned by the firm was on its way and it had barely turned the nearest corner before the larger engine followed in its wake. Such quick work by the works brigade is a credit to the firm as is also the new fire station. As the new premises have been previously described in our column it is unecessary to say any- thing further about them except that all the latest mechanical appliances have been used to facilitate the turning out of the brigade. The building was formally opened on Monday in the presence of the chief officers of the principal fire brigades in England, who came from as far south as London and AS far north as Edinburgh. I Many of these, especially the captains of small town brigades, went away, metaphorically green with envy, and wishing that they had the necessary funds at their disposal to bring their establishment equally up-to-date. But Messrs. Crosfield's preparations to cope with an outbreak of fire do not end with the provision of fire stations. In every department of the works the employes are trained and drilled until they attain a surprising degree of efficiency as firemen, and all hands are equally thoroughly instructed what to do in the case of an alarm. Every. building, nay every m room contains a supply of hosepes and fire buckets, with an abundant supply of water so that in the event of a fire immediate steps could be taken to prevent it spreading. In fact so extensive are the preparations that it would be a big outbreak which was not got under in a few minutee. The visitors, who numbered about 50, were greatly impressed by what they saw, and had nothing but praise to offer. After the somewhat fatiguing journey round the works on excellent tea was provided in the girls diningroom, followed by a little speechmaking. Captain GALLON on behalf of Messrs. Crosfields' extended a hearty welcome to the guests. The invita- tions were issued because of the keen interest which the firm took in the development of the fire service of the country. Had it not been that Dr. Markel was the vice-president of the North-Western District of the National Fire Brigade's Union he did not think they would have had such a pleasant afternoon. He desired that the fire chiefs present would be free in their criticism of the arrangements of the firm. " ." _ _ _

A vote of thanks, moved by Chief Officer DUN-BURY, Darwen, seconded by Chief Officer Etzrorr, Southport, and supported by Major Davies, Chester ; Captain Williamson, Chester; Superintendent Jones, Bolton; and Superintendent Bradburne, Lancashire and York shire brigade, was heartily accorded to Messrs. Crosfield for their hospitality. Mr. Elliott, of the firm of Messrs. Shand Mason later in the evening strongly advocated the establish, ment of better arrangement for mutual assistance between works and town lire brigades, lie did not see why there should be any jealousy between the two and thought that if they helped each other it would be to the benefit of all.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

THE MIDNIGHT SUN IN PARIS,

At the summer solstice in Paris, the sun de- scends only eighteen degrees below the horizon, and twilight continues from the setting of the sun in tha northwest until its rising in the north-east. At midnight a luminous arc several degrees high can be observed in the north. This faint light was first photographed by Pouchet and Quisset from the top of the Eiffel Tower, and it was conclusively proved to be from the sun. Photometric studyt was urged by the late M. C,ornu. A special photometer has now been constructed by M. Touchet, and with this appa- ratus the varying intensities of the twilight arc can be accurately measured from the Eiffel Tower Observatory.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
-- OIL AS FUEL.

Oil is as safe as any fuel can be, and its advan- tages are so great that it is rapidly taking a position in the industrial world, either to super- sede or to be used as the ally of coal. Crude oil is a perfect substitute for coal where the requisite arrangements have been made for its consump- tion. It is in the steamer that oil fuel displays its good qualities to the best advantage, and al- ready, in spite of heavy restrictions as to the flash point, enforced by Lloyd's. a remarkable extension of its use is being witnessed year by year. We have .one inducement to use oil fuel denied to other countries which have no oil fields, the Petroleum World points out, we admit it without tax or duty, and it is probably the cheapest fuel in the world in such circumstances.

VESUVIUS PAST AND PRESENT.

In the course of an interesting ar'ticle on the eruptions of Vesuvius, the Scientfie American remarks especially upon the astonighing escape of the Monte di Somma c:3isrnographic observa- tory. UNDERGROUND WIRELES4 TELEGRAPHY.-

A method of talking without the use of wires or other specially-installed conductors, has, after years of experiment, been invented by Father Joseph Murgas, •of the Slavonic Catholic Church, Wilkesbarre, Pa., who claims that be- fore long he will be able to send wireless mes- sages underground to Europe. For the purpose of , long-distance communication, a shaft 3.000 feet deep will be sunk in the United States. and a similar shaft in Europe. Each shaft will be walled with concrete to guard it from damp- ness; and wires leading some distance into the earth from the shafts will be encased in iron or steel tubing. High power will be required to generate the transmitting waves; and the in- ventor will use a special signal system of his own invention, which replaces the Morse signals used by other wireless systems, with musical tones, each indicating a letter of the alphabet in the code word or group of words, and thus giving- greater rapidity of operation than the Morse system. The Technical World Magazine of Chicago, which gives these particulars, adds that satisfactory results have attended tests with shallow shafts and small power, between points that are situated a. few miles apart.

MAMMAL FOSSILS FROM THE FAYOtiM.

The interesting results obtained by Dr. C. W. Andrews in his investigations of the fossil mat-il- l-paha of the classical region of Fayoum, in Egypt, have been published in the form of a descriptive catalogue by the trnstees of the Dritish Museum. The strata, from which the remains have been obtained are of middle and upper Eocene age, and. are situated to the north of that ancient irri- gation reservoir known as Lake Moeris. The more recent of these beds shew indications of having been deposited nearer the land mass, and the coast line advanced northwards as time passed en. One of the most interesting of the mammalian forms described is a four-toed ungu- late, with remarkably massive and powerful horns. Near the place where its bones were dis- interred from the rocks stands the mound repre- senting the site of Arsinoe, otherwise Crocodilo- polls, where the sacred crocodile kept in Lake Moeris was worshipped. Hence Dr. Andrews has appropriately named this strange creature Arsinoitherium, that is, the beast of Arsinoe. Lake Moeris, again, has provided a name for Moeritherium, interesting as the earliest and , most generalised representative of the Probosci- dian. or Elephant, order. Africa. which is one: of the two regions where elephants now occur, was thus probably the place of ()rigid of the order.

WANTED-A DEFINITION OF STEEL.

What is steel? This question of the definition of steel is as difficult to state as to refute; says the Magazine of Commerce, and on whatever lines the material is defined, on these same lines one meets with contradictions, and in- variably the argument is continued in a never- ending circle. Some authorities say that iron may be called steel, when, heated to, say, a cherry red and then 'suddenly cooled, it will re- sist the action of an ordinary file. If we take this as a definition, or define "steel" as a metal which can be hardened and tempered, and be made capable of producing a cutting edge, keen and lasting—which properties can be produced by certain alloys of iron and converted—then such material as that produced by the Bessemer and Open Hearth processes, and termed "mild steel," is really not " steel " at all, but an ex- alted form of wrought iron. Thus we can ar- gue this way and that, and contradict ourselves at every turn by the very arguments brought forward in support of the statements; and, whatever definition an expert may decide upon as the 'correct one for the material to be called "steel," he can find a metal which, according to his reasoning, may be called "steel," but is generally accepted as "iron," or may be called iron," when in reality it is "steel." Thus the

argument may be continued to any length, and, like the man drinking a light vintage wine, "get no forrader."

THE MICROSTRUCTURE OF BRASS.

In a paper bearing this title, published in the Journal of the Society of Chemical Industry, Mr. 0. F. Hudson gives an explanation of the , microscopic character of copper-zinc alloys con- taining more than 50 per cent. of copper. Brass containing 71 per cent, of copper, or more, consists of only one constituent—namely, a solid solution of zinc in copper—but the microstructure de- pends on the treatment the alloy has received. i The ordinary cast metal which s cooled fairly quickly has a definite dendritic structure owing to the heterogeneous nature of the crystals; an- nealing, however, allows diffusion to occur so that the structure becomes polygonal. The original crystalline structure is broken down and rendered granular by rolling. Subsequent an- nealing regenerates the crystalline structure, only, in this case, the crystals frequently exhibit twinning. This twinning is characteristic of the .material when annealed after, straining. Alloys containing 51-536 per cent. of copper shew no dendritic structure in the cast state, and no twinning on annealing. The structure of these brasses, and also of other samples of intermedi- ate composition, were illustrated by micro- photographs of specimens etched by rubbing on parchment moistened with dilute ammonia. This method of etching gave very good results. even although the colours of tfie constituents of .•eL 1 ions ...,15,ut ,en .1 Nu Burnt brass can be characterised by microscorA examination, for it has a coarse crystalline struc- ture and frequently contains small 'holes re- sena bling bubbles of gas.

THE EXAMINEIi,.

and should have full control over the Com- mittee in respect of the exercise of suck powers. Then uprose Mr, BALFOUR in great wrath. He appears to have made the most dramatic exhibition of himself that he has achieved since he returned to the House as member for the City of London, and as leader of a forlorn fragment of the Conservative party. The Government was faith- less and deceptive was his point of attack. It sprung important proposals like this on the House without notice and without previous intima tion of its intentions to its opponents. The House was being monstrously treated. The new proposal was an enormous change. It involved a new office, the creation of a staff. The Minister would require skilled advisers and a body of inspectors. Was ever such a thing heard of before ? Such was Mr. BALFOUR'S line of attack, pursued, to use Mr. Asourries happy phrase, in a temper of" artificially engendered passion." We all know how solicitious the present leader of the Opposition was in past times to keep faith with Parliament, to maintain its dignity, to sustain its supremacy over the Government. Under Mr. BALFoUR's regime the liberties of Parliament came very near extinction.

Bendict in the play says," When I swore that I would die a bachelor, I did not know that I should live to get married." When Mr. BALFOUR was Prime Minister, he did not know that he would live to become leader of a small Opposition, and that every weapon he forged against the liberties of Parliament might ultimately be used against himself. Yet, in spite of the violence of his protests, these weapons have not been so used. His passion was truly, as Mr. Asourra said artificially engendered. It was sound and fury, signifying nothing. The bubble was pricked quite successfully when it was remarked from the Ministerial Front Bench that the Government had merely adopted a suggestion from Mr. BALFOUR'S own aids. Accord- ing to the leader of the Opposition, such a pro- posal as that made by Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE has never before been placed before Parliament. He is mistaken—or something else. He will forgive us, perhaps, if we jog his memory. When he was Irish Secretary he got Colonel KING HARMAN appointed as his Under-Secretary in the House. It was a new position, it was an unauthorised position, it was a position with a salary attached. Mr. BALFoun's forgetfulness of this may have been magnificent, but it was not "the game."

In another column we CAPTAIN CROSFIELD are taken to task by AND THE CON- Captain G. R. CEOS' SCRIPTION SCARE. FIELD for our criticism of Lord ROBERTS and the conscription bogey,last week. Mr. CnosFrEtn's well .known craze for compulsory service is an ex- tenuating circumstance which somewhat excuses the intemperate nature of his criticism. 'We are innocent of any intention of"hurling taunts at the head of a gallant gentleman"—to wit Lordßonsars —and our admiration of him as a soldier and gentleman is quite as sincere as that of the most perfervid National Service Leaguer. But in coming to the front and endeavouring to create a public scare as to our national security from invasion, Lord ROBERTS invites his critics to forget his long lifetime of distinguished military service and to judge both the case and its advocate exactly as they appear. We repudiate warmly Captpig CROSFIELD'S implication that we have charged r/sfd ROBERTS with treason, and it is quite unneceSel for him to explain what treason is. Our only us° of the word—perhaps an unfortunate choice ei diction—last week was in the sentence which con eluded our lesding article, and read as follows Is it not almost treasonable, indeed, to try .to scare the country in such a manner as is really an invitation to our enemies ? " We merely ask a question here which, we confidently assert, nine people out of every ten would answer in the affirmative. Supposing Admiral Sir JOHN FISHER. w. ere to state from the highest public plat- form in the land that our navy was totally unprepared for war and would not have the ghost of a chance against a continental sea-power, would the nation thank hit', or would it condemn him for an indiscretion almost treason- able ? The circumstances of the real and the imaginary cases are outwardly on all fours, and Lord ROBERTS IS, to say the least, ill-advised in advertising our military strength and organisation as a travesty. Fortunately there is reason to believe that the army is not so black as it is painted, and ground also for real gratification in the fact that the recent proposals of the War Minister make for real efficiency in both our !regular and auxiliary forces. Captain CROSFIELD has put us on our defence and we would conclude it by saying that we do not abate one jot our con- demnation of men like Lord ROBERTS and Lord MILNER for endeavouring to scare the country into a form of compulsory service which is nothing but an insinuating first step to conscription. While the "blue water ' theory of defence holds good—and we do not see how there can be , any loss of faith in it so long as Britain remains an island—the tactics of the alarmists who would " dispense with the priceless boon of voluntary enlistment must be fought against unflinchingly. As the" Daily News" so convincingly argued on , July 11th, the day following Lord Roberts' speech in the House of Lords : " The dilemma is indeed unans.werable. So long as the fleet is triumphant no invasion of England is possible. If the fleet be defeated no defence of England is possible ; for in a few inert weeks the city populations could be starved into surrender The friends of conscrip- tion are. driven back to the rather forlorn . assertion that drill and discipline are good things in themselves, or to some vague and: general impression that if England were actually invaded our hordes of conscript defenders ' could put up a reputable resistance. Both these assertions depend upon an absence of that clear thinking which is one of the necessities of the hour. Drill is a good thing. Physical exercise is a good thing. Comradeship is a good thing. Patriotism is a good thing. The advocate of con- scription sees these things associated with coin- pulsory ssrvice. He jumps to the conclusion that they are the fruits of compulsory service. Few statements could be further from the truth. Con- scription necessarily exalts the soldier above the civilian. Instead of breeding patriotism it often excites passionate hatred of a cruel end clumsy system. In Germany those millions of Social Democrats—the flower of the working , class—are in active protest. It exalts the, mechanical, the routined. the blind qualities et obedience against all that is meant by hums° freedom. It may be a necessary thing. It can never be proclaimed a good thing. Is it then ,

ImM"

• , . '"

"necessary? Lord ROBERTS fell back upon the assertion that Great Britain had never depended upon her resources since the days :Henry VIII. and Elizabeth. But science—deliberate and inex- orable—has entered human affairs since those dis- tant days. Fleets and the morale behind the guns control the destinies of the seas. A nation which cannot feed itself has no use for its hungry con- reript hordes in the days of danger. There is no chance of this cry entering practical politics. Con- scription would be a far more fatal burden than Protection to any political party. An expansion of men trained in the use of arms, as a substitute for certain numbers of the regular Army, offers Possibilities of retrenchment and reform. Beyond this no militarist or alarmist is ever likely to Persuade this nation to venture." Warrington Liberals, to the A LIBERAL number of nearly 4,000 HOLIDAY. were at play on Saturday last. Under the auspices of the various associations which form the Liberal Party in the town there was a gigantic picnic to Southport, which was favoured with the brightest weather and thoroughly enjoyed. We think the Liberals of Warrington were entitled to the treat thus afforded them. They have this year achieved the greatest triumph obtained by any party in Warrington and now have the extreme felicity of being represented in Parliament a gentlemen who is not only very popular o-ut has in him the capacity for making a r`? useful member of the legislative assembly. dle,ll a triumph was not gained without a great of hard and persistent labour, and it was in , nature somewhat of a reward for their Political toil that the Liberals picnicked at Southport on Saturday, "The Member " was, O f course, present, and looking extremely " fit," to use a term which no one understands better than be. Though he hes not yet made his maiden , speech in the House, Mr. Crostield must have ' profited from listening to the torrent of eloquence i which never ceases there, for he delivered quitel the most fluent, and happily-conceived impromptu speech which we have heard him utter. He is already acquiring that indefinable but distinct I Parliamentary air characteristic of nearly all M.P.'s who are much in the House, and on Satur- day he spoke with a vigour and directness which has not always been his forte. We can heartily recommend his remarks to the perusal of our readers. It was a pleasure to note Mr. Crosfield's confidence in the Government's intention and ability to carry out eventually "every pledge given to the constituencies." There are "doubting Thomas's" who groan because the Government is compelled to hasten slowly, and to them Mr. Crosfield's oPtimism should come as a welcome relief. He Prophesies the carrying through in the autumn session of the amendment of the Workinen's Com- Pensation Act, and the Trades Disputes Bill, two Measures which have played a prominent part in the election argument here. The Government and he Commons are working, and working hard, for 'lnman progress and Warrington is proud to have a representative fully conversant with the needs of the moment and the ideals of the future.

I' The case arising out of .14IR, C. llloom- the will prepared' by the m' uorros WILL. late Mr. Charles Higgin- bottom a few days prior to his suicide on November 11th last, and which has occupied the Probate Court this week, possesses features of singular interest. Mr. Higginbottom oecame engaged to a Miss Turnock of Stockton Reath, less than a fortnight before his death and Made a will in her favour absolutely, thus setting aside a will dated July 1905, which settled a fixed Allowance on his mother, an old lady of SO. Pro- bate of the will in favour of Miss Turnock was °Pposed by Mrs. Higginbottom on the ground that when the testator made it, a few days before Committing suicide, ha was not responsible for his actions. fgood deal of the evidence called before Sir Gorell Barnes was in reference to the state of the deceased's mind at the time, sud • thong h there can be no doubt t .hat Arr. IL --gginbottom was then suffering from sOsomnia and melancholia we think the verdict of the Court un obi; -11---.ng his last will and testament is Perfectly justified. It has become a too common thing in this country for people in general and coroner's juries in particular to presume that because a man commits suicide he must be of unsound mind. A verdict offelo de se is far loss frequently given than the facts justify. A man rosy, while in full possession of his faculties' his reasoning powers, and even his mental balance, decide to quit this world. We cannot agree with , the contention of those people who say that by taking his own life he proves his montal unsound. ness. In the. case in question the evidence Shows that Mr. Higginbottom behaved in ? a perfectly rational manner—even if obviously ' Unwell—up to the time of making the will in con- '. sultation with his solicitor. It is not for the Pro- baterourt or anyone else to inquire into or doubt the sanity of the reasons which influenced him in flaking a will in favour of his sweetheart shortly ' oefore committing the dreadful act of self-destruc- tion. That he had reasons there can be no doubt, as he was clearly capable of reasoning at the ' time, no other verdict was, in strict justice, ' Possible. We are aware that much public sympathy extended to his mother, who is, apparently,thus i nenrived of the comforts due to her old age, but ' this fact does not affect the issue. Everybody , Will be unfeignedly glad, however, that the agree- ant.. made by the deceased's wish, and signed by v Niss Turoock, is to be carried out, so that the bereaved mother will be fitly provided for. Popular °Pinion will probably disagree with the verdict, but we have tried to show—what is our conviction that it was the only possible one under the °lellrnstances.

JULY 21st 1906.

The House of Commons I ARTIFICIAL seems again to have worked PASSION, itself into a heated condition on Tuesday. The subject was the Education Bill. Of course, secondarily, it was that portion of the Education Bill which proposes to establish an Educational Council for Wales. Mr. F. E. SMITH proposed a modification of the Government intentions. Mr. SMITH is the young gentleman whose maiden speech con- vinced all those who sit on the Opposition benches that the dormant talent of the Con- servative party had at length roused itself into life. He was a wit. He was even a genius, said the astonished and delighted Conservatives. He might even become a DISRAELI. The admiration so freely showered upon him has given Mr. SMITH confidence; but that is all it has done for him. It encourages him to speak, but be makes no more brilliant speeches. He may go down to history with another celebrated character, who, though he frequently addressed the House, was scornfully spoken of as " single-speech Hamilton." On Tuesday Mr. SMITH moved an amendment which would make it possible to appeal from the newly- constituted authority to the Board of Education. He wanted a closer and more intimate relation to Parliament. He desired to have control from Whitehall.

Mr. LLOYD•GEORGE, speaking for the Govern- ment, met him more than half way. Re agreed that there should be somebody in the House of Commons responsible for Welsh education, some- body to be " shot at," somebody who could be " riddled with paper pellets of the brain," through the medium of the notice paper. He need not necessarily be a paid Minister, but he would be responsible to Parliament for any act of the Welsh Central Committee done in the exercise of any of the powers of the Board of Education, dele- gated to the Committee under this section,

===== 0002601_19060721_art0064.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====

made from time to time by applicants before the Courts who have failed to obtain the certificate of exemption to which they con- sidered they were entitled. Obviously, from the ' tone of the circular, the Home Office believes there is some ground for the complaints. As we all know, the "conscientious objector" has any- thing but an enviable experience as a rule when he comes before the justices with an application for exemption. There is sure to be one or more magistrates on the bench who deem it their duty to heckle the applicant, of ten enough with the result that he gets so bothered that he fails to convince the beech that his objection is really a con- scientious one. Occasionally magistrates refuse to sign an exemption certificate because of a con- scientious objection of their own in the other direction. If they are not alone on the bench, the gentlemen who take this attitude are not greatly blameable, but if the coodurt of the business before that particular court is in their hands then they ought to be sharply reminded that they are sworn to administer the law as they find it however repugnant the task may be. The Home Office circular points out that "the question for magistrates is not whether the applicant's belief is well-founded or ill-founded, but solely whether they are satisfied that he conscientiously believes that vaccination will be prejudicial to the health of the child in question; the Act does not require that the belief should be reasonably founded. It is not necessary to the proof of a conscientious objection that a certificate should be produced, or even that evidence should be given, of any particular 'state of the health of the child," The circular concludes with the statement that Mr. Gladstone (Home Secretary) "has reason to believe that misapprehension in some cases of the meaning and intention of the Act has actually caused the friction which the provisions for exemption were designed to remove, and he fears that if this state of things continues the general operation of the Act will be prejudiced."

Exactly what has taken THE ATROCITIES IN place in Natal is known NATAL, neither to the Government in this country, nor, as it appears, to the Ministry in Natal. However, we shall be glad to hear from unimpeachable soucres that there is nothing worse than what was admitted to the House of Commons by Mr. Winston Churchill on Wednesday. We are not disposed to leave this subject without remarking on what appears to us to be a new and far from agreeable development of English character. Every Englishman surely should feel that the national honour is tarnished by anything which is instinctively condemned by civilised peoples. Yet there is nowadays a class of Englishmen ready to defend any atrocity if what they suppose to be British interests are concerned. Those who are anxious to maintain the national honour are described as the friends of every country but their own. In reporting the proceedings in the House of Commons on Wednesday afternoon, a leading organ of Toryism among the evening papers speaks of "various Ridical and other quarters " ' from which accusations have been made against the Natal native levies, Surely this is un-English ? At any rate, it is untrue, as the accusations were made public through the Conservative Press. But if there has been a refusal of quarter, if there has been a massacre of wounded men, if barbarian troops have acted like barbarians in fighting our battles, the shame that ensues should be common to Englishmen. We should not make a party matter of it. The fact that there are Englishmen. now existing who attempt to justify every method of warfare, and who accuse more humane men of want of patriotism because they believe that the British flag ought not to be associated with inde- scribable brutalities, would be a sign of monstrous national decadence if the stain went deep, as, how ever, we believe it does not.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The Education Committee established a record on Monday evening, the monthly meeting barely lasting ten minutes. * * * Mr. J. Ramsay Macdonald publicly describes the Hardie epistle as " rash." What he says of it in private conversation is not for publication, but it may be safely left to the imagination.—" Leeds Mercury ' correspondent.

The Examiner, Saturday, July 21, 1906. LABOUR AND THE POLICE.

* * * The continual increase in the number of window boxes in all parts of the town is a sure indication of the interest aroused and the (food done by the Beauti- ful Warrington Society. On September Ist. the society will hold its first annual show in Parr Hall. This week the annual show of the St. George's-in-the- East Window Garden Society has been held, no less than 738 plants being sent in by school children, and 180 by adults. We hope the Warrington Society will be able to equal these encouraging figures. The comfort which the leaders of the local Con- servative party are able to offer their followers just now is very " cold " indeed. At the " true, blue" picnic to Blackpool last Saturday words of remark- able wisdom and good cheer—they were accepted as such anyhow—fell from the lips of that eminent and far-seeing politician Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. " Judging from what they could see," he said " and from what they had been told by those in the know, they were not very far from another general election." (" Hurrah !" " loud cheers," " applause," etc.) We shall keep an eye on Sir Gilbert after this. He evidently knows something that no one else has any idea of, and we don't want him to blurt out the date of this imminent election at an inconvenient time.

He suggested that the chairman, Mr. Lacey, with Mr. Lancaster, the librarian, should confer with the other members and decide how the proposal could be carried out. Mr. Foote seconded the proposition, which was agreed to.

THE WAY TO OBTAIN ORDERS. INGENIOUS FRAUDS AT ST. HELENS.

Albert Prince, alias Taylor, tea canvasser, was charged at St. Helens, on Saturday, before Alderman J. C. Gamble and Alderman Forster with obtaining money by false pretences from Mrs. Adcock, if Eccleston Park, and also with acting as a pedlar without a certifi- cate.—Superintendent Baxendaie said that the man went to Mrs. Adcock's on Friday, after calling at a number of otheAresidences, and he handed Mrs. Adcock a ticket, and said, "I am not begging ; but I want you to do me a favour. I have got an offer of a situation with the Ceylon Growers' Association, of Oxford-street, Manchester, if I can get fifty orders in the neighbour- hood. Other people in the Park have been kind enough to give me orders and I am selling pure cocoa." Prince also said that he had worked for a firm at Liverpool, but had been thrown out of work by the reduction of the staff. He offered lb bags of cocoa at ss. each. After Mrs. Adcaok had agreed to buy the cocoa, Prince went to the Grapes Hotel, where he had left his bag, and someone informed Police-constable Guest of what the prisoner was doing, and he followed him, and took him into custody while he was delivering the cocoa.—Evidence was given that when prisoner was taken to Prescot, he admitted to Sergeant Bland that there was no such firm as the Ceylon Growers, I Association in existence, and that his story was untrue. Mr. Hemmingway, chemist, of Prescot, said that the cocoa that prisoner was selling was not worth more than 6d. per pound. Prisoner in his defence said that if he told the positive truth he could not get orders anywhere. He knew he was telling lies, but that was the usual wayof obtaining orders all over the country. A few months ago he was detained in a similar way at Liscard, but at the end of four or five days the magis- trates discharged him, and that made him think that he was not doing wrong. The Chairman : You have been in prison several times for the same thing. You have done it all over the country, and must go to gaol for 1 three months' hard labour.—Prince was again brought before the St. Helens borough magistrates on Monday on two similar charges. Evidence was given that on February 2 Prince went to the residence of Mr. Cook, The Sycamores, Toll Bar, and on the pre- tence that if he could secure fifty orders in the neighbourhood he would get .a permanent I position with a fictitious Indian and Ceylon company at Manchester, he got Miss Cook to take a packet of cocoa for 2s. 6d., which Dr. Buchan, the medical officer, said was not worth more than Gd. Dr. Buchan said it was not genuine cocoa but was mostly arrowroot starch. On the same date he sold Miss Willcock, of Ravensdale, two pounds of the cocoa for ss.—The magistrates sent Prince to gaol for two months on each charge, the four months to follow the period for whichhe was sentenced on Saturday.
