The Widnes Conflict.

The dispute between the Widnes police and the Independent Labour party as to the right to hold meet- ings in the Victoria Square did not reach so great a height as bad been expected on Saturday night, when about 1,600 assembled to listen to a speech by Mr. J. A. Seddon, the Labour member for the Newton division. At the start the police were not to be seen. Mr. Seddon asked was this a truce or a calm before a storm, or an indication that their assailants had discovered they were on the wrong tack? (Cheers.) A week ago his name was taken by the police, but up to the present he had received no indication that be was to be a guest of Edward VII. for seven or fourteen days.—(Cheers.) They were law-abiding citizens gathered for the defence of free speech and to maintain that no authority had any power to interfere with peaceable citizens so long as traffic was not impeded. They would continue these meetiags ; coa- tinue to lay before the people a new set of political principles which would prevent this country entering upon revolution, which was always destructive to the class to which they belonged. (Cheers.) At this point the police appeared, and a sergeant asked Mr. Seddon to go away. Mr. Seddon : As soon as I have finished we'll go. You know my name.—(Cheers.) The Sergeant : I shall have to report you.— ( Ho oting . ) Mr. Seddon (to the meeting) : Fair play. I era prepared to defend myself against anything this gentle- man can do. So let us have a peaceable meeting. Mr. Councillor Boyle (chairman of the meeting) was also told that he would be reported as usual. Mr. Seddon : "'As usual; it's a standing dish with him."—(Laughter). The police then went away. Mr. Seddon presumed that nest Thursday they would enter the police court and afterwards another building where there was quiet solitude and no crowds to dis- turb, and where the diet led to high thinking. The cause of free speech, however, must not suffer from spite nor political tyranny. "Personally," he "I have no property to seize; my wife, who Is a better housekeeper than I am, owns the lot. Talk about penal servitude, Portland would be far more agreeable than listening to the yapping that goes on night after night in the Hous) of Commons. Don't think I shall look upon myself as a martyr. I shall ** * consider it a very pleasant rest-cure from the turmoil The traffic question over Stockton Heath Swing of Parliamentary life and open-air meetings. 13 utif Bridge is sure to try the patience of the future genera- they silence my voice for a short time, like Punch,' 1. shall be here again maintaining the right of free tion if it is not grappled out by the present one. The -s speech." number of vehicles and the variety of their character ; pThe large crowd then separated quietly. to say nothing of the number of pedestrians who are ; stopped occasionally owing to the passing I REPORTED AMICABLE COMPROMISE. of the vessels along the Ship Canal is at times An effort is being made to end the unfortunate con- amazing. On Monday last,quite an ordinary day for test/ between the Widnes police and the local branch of traffic for em bxaple, at one stoppage of the ridge on the Independent Labour party, who hold public meet- ings in Victoria-square. A Widnes correspondent under. the Stockton Heath side there was to be seen a tram stands that an interview has taken place between the car, milk cart, fish cart, three potato carts, ginger responsible persons on either side, and suggestions have beer cart and a cab, to say nothing of the usual drays, been made which it is confidently hoped will result in empty carts and cycles returning to town, which all arrangements being made that will be satisfactory to appeared to make a strange blend and of course took all concerned. up almost the whole of the road, from the bridge past ; the church. The brightest feature about it perhaps, I was the symptoms of prosperity. * * It has been said by some observer of human character that we all have our 'peculiar idiosyncrasies.' Well, there is a certain young minister within 100 miles of Warrington who feels deeply insulted if he is not addressed in person or by letter as "Reverend." He has been taking his friends to task for omitting of late this title to his name. That is his "idiosyncrasy." There is another well-know gentleman in the same district who objects to be announced as So and so Esq., and prefers the plain Mr. This is his "idiosyn- crasy." However both of these have been beaten by a certain cricketer who having a letter addressed to him as S.) and So Esq., which title was evidently something so novel and pleasing to him,((or he did not move in a high social position) that he carried the letter in the envelope about with him, placing it in his pocket which also contained his handkerchief. He often used the latter nasal requirement as he strutted about the cricket field with the result that the letter often fell upon the ground. The letter would then be found by someone and brought to the pavilion and handed to the would-be-gentleman to whom it belonged. This occurred so often, that the cricketer's "idiosyncrasy " was discovered and his conceit and vanity were the cause of much hilarity, to say nothing of the comtempt of his comrades.

* * * Mr. Vadclison, the member for Burnley, has sent a cutting reply to the Birmingham and Pitovincial Gun- makers' Association, who have circularised members of Parliament with respect to the Spurious Sports Bill and its bearing on their trade. He says : —"You ask me to oppose the Spurious Sports Bill. Why? Because it will injure the gun trade. But so does the continuance of peace. Really, eVen a member of Parliament is entitled to something better than this to be addressed to him. When the slaughter of pigeons and small birds has to seek its apologists in gunmakers its end is surely near. The nearest parallel to your trade circular that 1 can think of is the petition of the silversmiths of Ephesus, whose devotion to Diana increased as their trade was threatened by the teach- ings of St. Paul. Your Association might have fol- lewed a better lead.

ST. HELENS WOMAN'S DEATH.

A Serious Case.

INQUEST FORMALLY ADJOURNED.

Mr. Brighouse, the county coroner, reopened the inquest at St. Helens Town Hall, on Monday with respect to the death of Ruth Dinah Gibson, married woman, aged forty-oneof 97, Hoole-bank, who died in the Providence Hospital, St. Helens, on Saturday night June 30. The Coroner said the question was whether this woman's death was attributable to the wrongful and illegal act of Annie Taylor, a midwife, who attended her in ber confinement on the 9th of the same month. He inquired if Mrs. Taylor was legally represented. Mrs. Taylor, an old woman of about seventy, came forward and said she had not got a solicitor. The Coroner addressing Mrs. Taylor said :—This is a very serious matter for you if it is in consequence of your illegal and wrongful act this poor woman has come by her deith,and the question'will be for the jury to consider whether they ought to send you for trial on a charge of mansllughter. You ought to be represented by a solicitor. I have had an opportunity of reading the proofs of witnesses, and I assure 701.1 that in my opinion it is a very serious matter indeed for you. The Coroner intimated that, although it would be of great inconvenience to the jury and to himself, he would adjourn the inquiry in order to give Mrs. Taylor another opportunity of getting legal assistance. He pointed out to her that she would have to pay for this assistance. _ .

Mrs. Taylor inquired how much it would cost. The Coroner: Although I am a solicitor myself, I conic not very well tell you. The price varies very considerably. The market value is an unknown quantity. They are like boots ; you can get them at any price. The Coroner then formally adjourned the inquest until next Monday morning. It appears that on June 9th, Mrs. Gibson was con- fined of twins, and she was attended by Mrs. Taylor, Mrs. Gibson afterwards began to show serious symptoms and on the 23rd of June Dr. O'Keeffe was called in and found her in a bad conditien. He reported the matter to the health authorities, and Mrs. Taylor WAS called on to appear before the Health Committee, when they decided to report the case to the Central Midwives' Board. In the meantime Mrs. Gibson died in the hospital, and the coroner was communicated with.

WARRINGTON

Index to Inside Pages.

By the death of Joseph Finch, famiarly known as "Blind Joe "a well known Warrington character has been removed. Deceased, who was 34 years of age, was at one time a familiar figure in the Warrington streets where he earned a precarious livelihood by selling papers. He was perhaps better known, how- ever as a football enthusiast. He rarely missed a match at Warrington and frequently followed the first team to other Lancashire towns finding his way about with an ease which,considering his infirmity was truly remarkable. _ _

PREMIER CYCLES, 7,8, 10, 12 and 14 guineas Cash or 18 monthly payments. Sole agent, J. S. GARLAND, 51, 53 and 55, Bridge-st.—Advt. Liberal Voters Please Note! The new list of voters for 1906 will be published on August Ist and affixed to the door of every place of worship in the Borough, and on and after that date ' it can also be seen at the Liberal Club, Bewsey-street. Every Liberal who has been a householder for 12 ' months ending July 15th last, should examine the list for himself, and if his name is omitted or if there is any error or misdescription of any kind whatever, he should inform Mr. J. Howes, jun., the Liberal agent, at once, so that the vote may not be lost. Lodgers who occupy one or more rooms of the clear value of 3:10 are also entitled to vote, and should claim imme- diately. Women who are householders should also see that they are registered in division three (municipal list). Claim forms and all assistance will be furnished on application at the Registration offices, Liberal Club Warrington. All claims must be served upon the the overseers on or before August 20th. 5, R. Wool:), Piano Dealer and Toner, 47, Old London-road., Stockton Heath.—Advt. Carden Party at Hall Nook, Penketh. On Monday afternoon the members of the British Women's Temperance Association went by omnibus to Hall Nook, Penketh, where they were kindly received by Mrs. Garnett, who has acted as President or Vice. President of the Association since its com- mencement in 3une,1893. Some time was spent in the spacious garden and numerous greenhouses,the;herbace- oils growth being much admired, also the great variety of sweet-scented roses. The arbours formed a refresh- ing resting glace from which the lovely picture could be semi. The conservatory, which was full of rare and beautiful flowers, was also a pleasant resort. An enjoy- able time was spent in the house where tea was served. Amongst those present were the Rev. J. Gunnell, Miss James, Mrs. Withinshaw, and Mrs. Parker. Afterwards a short meeting was held when Mrs. Simpson, of Southport, who was on a visit to Miss Bessie Dutton, gave a pleasing description of the grand pageant which she had witnessed in Warwick and which was held during the first week of July. Mr. Louis N. Parker was the author and organizer of the historic pageant which was got up chiefly for the glorification of Warwick and was visited by people from the four quarters of the globle. The tableau history of events of 2,000 years lived again before an andiencQ of 5,000 spectators f a- six days in succession. The magnificent display took place within the grounds of Warwick Castle, which are very extensive. The immense lawn with its setting of fir,'beech and hanging hazel formed a lovely picture with the Avon winding away to the background of woods from the centre of which ran a green road up a steep hill. The pageant was performed entirely by. men and women of the country; people of high and , low degree bad worked heart and soul in preparing, making the dresses and properties and devoting much' time to rehearsing. The story of the past had rolled I by with the, dignity and grace of an old-world tale,l from the day long prior to Ethelfleda's founding of the castle to the present. The gorgeous spectacle was brought to. a close when the strains of the National Anthem floated out, and the 2,000, actors pcsing in position and the 5,000 spectators standing within the great stand was a scene never to be forgotten. An excellent report of the annual meetings of the 8.W.T.A., which were held in London was giver by Mrs. Simpson, hon. secretary to the society. Of..icers for the ensuing year were re-elected when Mrs. Garnett proposed that Mrs. Holines,Rugemont,Penketh be appointed president. Miss Bessie Dutton thanked Mrs. Garnett for her kind hospitality and Mrs. Garnett :thanked the two Mrs Simpsons for their interesting reports. Again the rose garden was , visited when each one with a handful of roses formed , a circle round a large .lied of roses and sang," Praise • God from whom all blessings flow." Soon after, the omnibus was boarded and conveyed the party to town.

Accident at School-Brow. At about 5.30 on Monday afternoon, Lily Holbrook, the eleven-year-old daughter of Thomas Holbrook, 22, John-street, was playing against a large sliding yard door at the back of a milk shop belonging to Mr. Edward Roberts, oberts 58, School-brow, when t fell upon her. Her legs were badly bruised and she was taken to the residence of her grandmother, where Dr. Ander- son attended to her injuries.

UNDER THE CLocic for Good Boors. Advt.— Warrington P.S.A. here was a good attendance a t the weekly meeting of the Warrington Society held in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Sunday. Mr. J. C. Holland presided, and in the course of a short address stated that the P.S.A. and kindred societies had done much to maintain the sacredness of our English Sunday. It would be a sad day for old England if anything was allowed to impair the sacredness of our Sabbath. (Hear, hear.) Mr. T. L. Robins delivered an address on " Courtesy," pointing out that it was the duty of every aristian to be courteous. There was no religion in bluntness and rudeness. Mr. P. Barber read the Scripture lesson. Prayer was offered by Mr. R. Nelson, and Mr. E. Wynne Jones also took part in I the service.

GARLAND, 51,Bridge-st.—Advt. ' Theft cf Potatoes. At the Warrington Police Court this (Friday) morning, John Baguley, greengrocer's hawker, was committed to gaol for three months for stealing four bags of potatoes value 10s., the property of John Lomax. It appears that on Wednesday Lomax attended the market at Stockton Heath and purchased a load of potatoes. Having to go to Earlestown, and finding the load too big he left eleven bags in the Norton Arms yard, Baguley helping to unload them. Returning next day he discovered four bags were missing, and ascertained that prisoner had sold them to a Mrs. Green, Knntsford-road, for 10s.—Baguley made his (31st appearance. Music in the Park. At the Warrington Occasional Court, on Thursday morning, Samuel Simpkiss and James Greenwood, two youths, hailing from Ashton-under-Lyne, were charged with breaking and entering the shop of William Court, Padgate-lane, Poulton, near Warrington, and stealing a number of articles of jewellery and other property. On the night of the 25th ult. the premises were safely seciired by Mr. Court, but on the following morning he found the place had been entered, an entranca having been effected by the back window. The house was in a state of disorder, the drawers ransacked, and the jewellery and other articles missing. The stolen property was afterwards traced to Asht m-under-Lyne, where prisoners were arrested by Sergeant Mellor. Prisoners were committed to the Liverpool Assizes. Messrs Crosfield and Woolwich Arsenal. On Tuesday a party from Woolwich Arsenal visited the works of Messrs. Joseph Crosfield and Sons Ltd., at Bank Quay and made an exhaustive inspection with a view to reporting on the arrangements for the economical consumption of fuel. The authorities have been impressed by the repeated statements in the Press as to the large saving which this firm has effected by its improved methods and appliances and the consequence was this deputation which represented three different departments. This is not the first visit to the works from the Woolwich Arsenal, for some time ago a deputation was sent to inquire into the views and practice of the firm, among others, in educational matters. It was pleasing to I hear the other day that the Government authorities at Woolwich had remodelled their regulations exactly on the same lines as Messrs. Crosfield and with the most satisfactory results. Conservative Picnic. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

MR. SEDDON, M.P., FINED.

The Widnes Free Speech Crusade.

GAOL BEFORE PAYING.

Mr. James A. Seddon, M.P. for the Newton Division and twenty members of the Independent Labour party were summoned at Widnes on Thursday for obstructing Victoria square by holding meetings there. 'Mr. Seddon denied that there was obstruction in the ordinary sense. By custom the Square was the popular rendezvous for public meetings of a religious and political character and to put the Highway Act in operation was strain log of the law that would arouse in the minds of the people a bitterness that boded ill for law and order. , Until the Corporation came to some arrangement these meetings would be continued. He did not want to come into conflict with the police, but he would defend free speech whatever the consequence might be. Fines of half-a-crown and costs were imposed. Several declared their intention not to pay. Mr. Seddon had departed before the decision was given, but a few days ago he declared he would go to I prison rather than pay.

Last Thursday at Newton Petty Sessions Thomas Aldred pleaded guilty to stealing coal at Grolborne.— Mr. A. Ellis, for Messrs. Richard Evans and Co., said 1 this was a more serious case than usual, because Aldred I was a boiler foreman in the Company's emyloy and might be expected to set a better example. On June 29th, nearly midnight, P.C. Hayhurst met him and his wife, carrying three large lumps of coal which defen- I dant threw down in his own yard. Witness followed him, and he admitted having taken the coal from Messrs. Evans' siding, saying, "I'm very sorry ; it's the first time I've been caught." Fined 105. and costs.— Ellen Evans and Mary Owen Jones were summoned for a similar offence at Haydock. The former appeared, and a warrant was ordered to be issued to bring up Mrs. Jones.—Mrs. Evans as fined 10s. including sosts.

SMOKING IN A "NON-SMOKER." John Lyons' of 39, Jackson-street, Manchester, was summoned at Newton on Thursday last, for a breach of the railway bye-laws, by smoking in a non-smoking compartment, at Newton, on April 21.—His father appeared for him.—Mr. Forma, prosecuting for the L. and N.-1.17. Railway Company, said the defendant and another man, 'who travelled from Manchester by 8-2 p.m. train, made themselves obnoxious to other people travelling with them, by bad language smoking, &c. Complaints were made and the railway officials removed the two men to another compartment. Lyons gave his I correct name and address, but the other man did not, and had not been traced.—Frederick Jenkinson, porter at Newton, confirmed Mr. Fenna's opening, and said he removed the two men to another compartment.— Nathaniel Conway, guard, also corroborated, and a rail- way detective deposed that when he called on the defendant Lyons he said he did not remember anything except that he was very drunk, and was removed from one compartment to another.—Fined 10s. and costs.

ST HELENS.

CBIIIEAN VETERAN'S DEATH AT HELENS.

The death has taken place, in the St. Helens Provi- dence Hospital, of John Falloon, aged seventy, a Crimean veteran, who a few days ago narrowly escaped death by drowning whilst bathing in the sea at Black- pool. He was rescued in an apparently lifeless con- dition, and was only brought round after prolonged efforts at artificial respiration. The immersion appar- ently seriously affected his health, for upon returning to St. Helens he became very ill, and succumbed at the hospital.

ENLARGEMENT OF THE ST. HELENS MUSEUM Mr. A. Lancaster, curator of the St. elens Corpora- tion Museum, which is situated in the mansion at the Victoria Park, on Wednesday presented an interesting report on museum matters, and pointed out that at the rate the St. Helens collection is growing it will soon be necessary to use one or more of the rooms on the first floor, nearly all the available space on the ground floor of the mansion being taken up. He suggests that the offer of the Victoria and Albert Museum authorities of loan collections should be accepted. Mr. Lancaster also reported a valuable gift from the trustees of the British Museum of a set of electro-types of British historical medals.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

BURGLARIFS AT WARRINGTON.

. - At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday John Flannaghan, described as a fitter's labourer,was charged on remand with stealing from the dwelling-house of Thomas Hagan, 37, Eldon-street,some time between two p.m. and five p.m. on Thursday, July 12th,a pair of men's boots, value 6s, 6d. He was further charged with steal- ing from the office of 'Tesoro. T. Looker and Co., Ellesmere-street, between nine p.m. on Friday,July Ist, and 6.30 a.m. on Saturday, Jnly 2nd, a pocket knife, a steel tape measure, a leather pouch containing steel tools, a box containing a gross of steel pens, and four postage stamps, of the total value of 149.—Superin- tendent Monaghan stated that Thomas Hagan went to his work in the morning in question leaving his wife in the house, and at 2.30 she also had occasion to leave, and she locked up the premises. When she returned at 5.30 she noticed that a small square of glass had been cut out of the kitchen window and that her husband's boots had been stolen. Prisoner's house was searched, with the result that the boots were discovered.—With regard to the second charge, the Superintendent stated that Messrs. Locker's clerk locked the office up on the Ist inst., and when he returned the next morning at 6.30 he found that it had been broken into, and an entrance effected by breaking a large square of glass in the window. One drawer had been broken open with a crowbar. A quantity of blood was found on the window and on the desk, and he missed some postage stamps, pens, etc. Information was given to the police, and the prisoner was arrested and a penknife was found in his ' possession which answered the description of the one I missing from Messrs. Locker's premises.—The prisoner who pleaded guilty,said : " Whatever sentence you see fit to pass upon me, I shall take it."—The Chairman described the offence as a serious one, and committed prisoner to gaol for three months with hard labour.

A QUEER SUCCESSOR TO MISS GARVEN. At the Borough Police Court, Warrington, on Saturday, an elderly woman, named Sarah Ann Fast,was charged with sleeping out in a large unoccupied house In Sankey-street,which had been purchased by the Cor- poration for the purpose of street improvement and was formerly tenanted by Miss Garven.—P.C. Holland said that he was on duty in Sankey-street at seven o'clock that morning when his attention was called to smoke which was issuing from a chimney. He went into the house where he saw the woman sitting near a large fire. She was making herself very comfortable, and looked like stopping for some time.—Superinten- dent Monaghan stated that the woman went from empty house to empty house, and took cooking utensils with her, and made herself quite at home. She had been in the house in question for some days.—The Chairman (Mr. J. Hayward) : She has evidently aristocratic tendencies. (Laughter.) She will have to go to a still larger house for seven days.

... .. STARCH

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

An easy victory fell to Warrington in their League match at Newton on Saturday. Our local wielders of the willow are showing capital form at present and if it is maintained there should be no cause to be ashamed of the season's work. The Newton batsmen could make no headway against the deliveries of E. A. Connor and Crewmen who worked great havoc. The innings lasted barely an hour and realized the poor total of 43. Connor and Creswell were irresistible, capturing 5 wickets for 19 runs and 5 for 25 respectively. Duck- worth brought off three smart catches.

The Warrington start was anything but promising, several batsmen being dismissed for poor sores. E. A. Connor and Cresswell put a different complexion on the game, both hitting out in splendid style. Connor, knocked up 89 which included one six and five fours while the professional carried out his bat for a fault- lessly compiled £4, his chief hits being eight fours. The innings closed for 158, giving Warrington a splendid victory.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0082.txt | included=true | included =====
The most successful bowler for Newton was Howarth who took four wickets for 29 runs.

Earlestown had no difficulty in defeating Prescot and still head the League with 15 points to their credit. Warrington come next with 13 points.

Warrington second triumphed over their Newton visitors and the victory was very creditable to them although their opponents were three men short. Batting first Newton were all out for 163 the features of the innings being the fine displays by F. Harrison and T. Heppell who made 56 and 47 not out respectively. Warrington were in a great scoring mood and ran up 213 for the loss of nine wickets. There were several notable individual efforts. E. E. Woods made a brilliant 68, hitting twelve fours. A. Thewlis batted splendidly for his 54 while H. Dolan's 43 included five boundaries.

St. Pant's achieved a victory at the expense of Sankey Wire fills 2nd, their majority being 41. H. Booth batted in highlv creditable style, 24 of his 27 being compiled in boundaries.

Latchford Baptists accounted for Pemberton • St. Cuthbert's with a 25 margin.the most sucessful batsman being. J. Stowell, who knocked up 14.

Sankey Juniors defeated Fairfield, the top scorers being Bradbum (25) and H. Daintith (16).

Moore and Daresbury very severely trounced Greenall Whitley's who put up 21 against the villazers' 113. For the home team A. Dutton and H. Taylor batted in splendid form, both making 32. H. Taylor and H. Brough bowled effectively,Taylor taking six wickets for 17 runs and Brough two wickets for one run. J. Wray was the only man on the visitors' side who attained double figures.

St. James's 2nd met Bewsey-road Wesleyan 2nd and ran out winners by 31. There was no tall scoring, Mr. Aukland (11) making the best effort.

Bewsey-road Ist fared more fortunately, accounting for St. James's by a majority of 8. F. Tanner batted splendidly for his 25.

The Works Compet;tion proceeds apace, and the third round has been entered upon. On Monday evening the second round closed with a match between Cros- fields and" Guardian," the Soapery men being easy winners. Crosflelds batted first, and during their fifty minutes stay amassed 82, E. W. Warren carrying ant his bat for 27. Useful contributions were also made by Atherton and T. Whitfield. The Printers fired badly against the deliveries of Dickinson and Gleave, and were dismissed for the small score of Li. Dickinson took five wickets for seven runs.

The third round opened on Tuesday evening, when Walker's Brewery played Crosfield's before a large crowd. The victory rested with the Brewery eleven, who in response to -Crosfleld's 45 made 68 for the loss of 8 wickets. Norman captured seven wickets for 17 runs.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

1 What promises to be the biggest main of the year is being organized in connection with the Warrington 'District of the Independent Order of Oddfellows, Man- chester Unity. It was originally proposed that an inter-Lodge League should he formed, bnt this was found to be impossible, as the bowling strength of each Lodge is an unknown quantity. The proposal has not, however, been allowed to drop, and the main, which is open to the whole district, is being held with a view to unearthing the trundling talent of the district. Play will commence on August nth over the green attached to the Rope and Anchor Inn, Woolston, and as an entry of about 400 is confidently anticipated, the proceedings should prove highly interesting.

The second round in connection with the Works Corn- petition was concluded on Saturday, and the third round will open to-day. Two of Saturday's matches call for little comment inasmuch as the victorious teams always held the whip hand. They were Sankey Wire Mills, who accounted for P. and K. (3) and P. and K. (1) who as expected had little difficulty in disposing of Robinson's.

The other two games were not won without a pro- longed struggle. When they embarked upon their venture with Penketh Tannery, Richmonds' confidently looked for victory and an opening lead of 15-3 inspired them with confidence. They did not lose heart when two games later they were in arrears but when W. Ashcroft lost to J. Lloyd matters became serious. J. Smith did not inspire confidence with his opening as he allowed Shane to run from 9-0 to 11-4. He however made a fine effort and ran home before Shane could again score, and two points only separated the teams. Bennett was always too good for J.Hall and Richmonds' went to the front with the totals standing at 73-70.

A hard-fought game between A. Jordan and J. Atkin- on, resulted in the discomforture of the gas stove man and the score was carried to 86-85 in honour of Richmonds'. From this point the tide turned and flowed against the favourites as E. Bond, having a splendid win over E. Fletcher gave his side the load. Strive as they could Richmonds could not again get to the front and after a game fight they retired beaten by eight points.

Whitecross put up an equally good fight against Rylands. Opening well, they ran from 154 to 86-83 and then to 101-93. Then hopes of victory ran high but some uneasiness was caused when T. Ellison lost to J. Shaw 10-17 i and their lead was reduced to one point. Thoir prospects again improved and witit the tenth man off the green visions of success loomed large before their eyes. They had almost commenced an anticipatory cheer when the • fall came. This was brought about by J. Wells who ran away from E. Cleave 15-9 and reversed the lead. Whitecroas still hoped that their last man would pull the match out of the fire but they were doomed to disappointment and Rylands qualified for the third round by a margin of 10 points.

To-day's list of matches will be found on an inside page.

DEATH AT HIS POST.

James Meadows, aged fifty-one, of Phythian-street, St. Helens, watchman at Messrs. Pilkington's timber works, was found dead on Saturday morning shortly before six o'clock. Meadows had occupied this post for a good many yenrs,and was a well respected servant of the company. He had attended to his duties as watchman during the night in the usual way, but appeared te have been seized with illness, and when the other watchman came on at six o'clock he found him dead.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, July 21, 1906.

A HUGE PICNIC.

WARRINGTON LIBERALS INVADE

SOUTHPORT.

It is no novelty for Warrington to send forth. an army of day trippers to invade some seaside resort. Excursions are common enough at this time of the year and the dwellers in this busy old town of ours are not slow to avail themselves of the opportunity of spending pleasant hours amidst more congenial sur- roundings. The great Liberal picnic to Southport on Saturday, however, easily established a record in point of dimensions. Special trains poured into the increas- ingly popular resort a host of 3,600 Warring- II tomans bent on extracting the maximum of enjoy- I ment from the outing—and enjoy themselves they undoubtedly did. It was indeed a case of" Warring- ton by the sea "and one could hardly turn a street corner without coming across batches of local trippers. Moreover, all were delighted with the visit. The day was gloriously fine. The sun shone radiantly while Ithe heat was tempered by a refreshing breeze. There were animated scenes at the Central Station, Warring- ton, during the morning and early afternoon. As the I time of departure drew near the platform was filled from end to end with 'a surging mass of humanity. Four special trains had been engaged And -they were dispatched between the hours of 12.20 and 2 o'clock Many hundreds of trippers,however, went by ordinary Itrains before the greatest rush began. Mr. Pinion, Imanager of the Cheshire Lines Committee, personally superintended the arrangements Ind watched the departure of the " specials." It was indeed a great multitude to accommodate. Representatives of all the local Liberal organizations mustered in strong force. There were members of the Liberal Association, Women's Liberal Association, Progressive Labour League, and Junior Liberal Aasociation—all of which have done yeoman service in the progressive cause in Warrington. Those who left Central Station at two o'clock enjoyed the privilege of travelling in a new train, fitted up in the most modern style, which had never been used before. Of course, as each train steamed into the station there was some lively crush- ing and struggling to board the carriages, but all managed to find room and Sonthport was reached in good time. Most Warringtonians are familiar with the beauties of this favourite resort whose handsome, tree-lined streets and pretty gardens give it an air of superiority all its own. The only disadvantage of the place is that one has to be con- tent to view the sea at a distance, although the Cityl Fathers have tried to give Southport more of the typical seaside appearance by constructing a marine lake. The Warringtonians lost no time in sampling the hosts of attractions of which the town boasts. They were to be seen careering along the fine marine drive in motor cars, indulging in the excit- ing delights of the water chute, flying machine, and helter-skelter lighthouse, and liberally patroniz- ing the many other devices to amuse the visitor and induce him to part with surplus pence. Rides on the car to the lovely gardens at Church- town and the equally pleasant Hesketh Park also proved popular modes of recreation. Arrangements had been made for tea to be provide at the Winter Gardena and thither the multitude wended their way between the hours of 3.30 and 6 o'clock. Hr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., who had come specially from London in order to meet the Warringtonians, visited each of ithe tea rooms in turn and was very cordiallyreceived. Amongst others present were 'Mr. C. E. Parker (president of the Liberal Association), Councillors Evans and Ellison, Mr. and Mrs. J. Howes, Hr. J. Howes, inn. (hon. secretary of the Trip Cammittee), Mr. William Furness (hon. treasurer), and other prominent workers. To cater for 3,600 is no light task and when the bulk of the 3,600 want to be fed at once there is reason for a certain amount of confusion. The arrangements did not give the utmost satisfaction but there was no alternative but to make the best of the situation. It had been the intention to hold a meeting after tea in the Alb-rt Hall but at the last moment the organizers were informed that this build- ing was not available. An open-air meeting was held and interesting speeches (reported below) were delivered by the Borough member and others. The trippers then adjourned to spend the few remaining hours as inclination dictated and in the cool of the evening it was exceedingly pleasant to ramble about the shore. The first excursion train left for home at 8.30 and others followed at intervals, the last reaching Warrington at 11.35. All voted the outing to have been a complete success and Saturday will always bs recalled as a day of real pleasure under delightful conditions. A word of praise is due to Mr. J. flowes, jun., who was largely responsible for the arrange- ments for the trip.

ADDRESS BY MR. A. H. CHOSFIFLD, M.P.

The Work of the Government. " Every Pledge to be Carried Oat."

At the shore open-air meeting held in the Winter Gardens after tea,Mr. A. ft.'Crostield M.P., delivered an address. He took the opportunity of expressing his hearty thanks to all those who played an active part in the great contest they had in Warrington which had, if be might so express himself eraanoipated the town from a kind of politics which he honestly thought was dominant a very great deal too long. He had never been one of those who thought that Liberalism was a short cut to Utopia—be was afraid he had seen enough of the commercial and political world to realize that 'there was no short cut to Utopia—but he had always I had a conviction, which only deepened the longer he was associated with politics, that British Liberalism ; was the best factor in a well ordered and sure, if slow, human progress. He bad also felt that while Liberalism was a grand thing for the I population of the United Kingdom, it also helped us Britons to take a lead in the world and give other nationalities a chance of working out their salvation on the same lines. Therefore, he thought that all who took part in that great contest might have a deep conviction that they had been lighting for great principles. (Applause). He did not want to sty anything unkind of the many Conservative friends that he had in 1 Warrington. He knew that his family had been accustomed to call a spade a spade and not to mince matters—(bear, hear)—and all his Tory friends knew quite well what he thought of the long era of

TORYISM AND JINGOISM that we had had. He was afraid it was very much at a discount just now; so that it was rather in pity than in anger that they did refer to their unfortunate political opponents. (Laughter). They (the Conservatives) were counting—so they told him—on what was called the swing of the pendulum. Well, that pendulum swung a long time in one direction, and they would find it as going to swing a long time now in another direction. (Applause). Their opponents were attempting to make la lot of capital out of the fact that the Government could not run a dozen omnibuses abreast through Temple Bar or. to drop metaphor, that they could not undo all the mischief that had been done during the last twenty years—with a very brief interval—in the first Session of Parliament. That was not the occasion to enter into details, but when he had the privilege of addressing not a pleasant holidayaudience like they had that day, out political gatherings in the autumn, he had not the slightest fear o the account that one would be able to give of the work that the Government was doing. (Applause). He could give them quite a list of 'metalwork in the way of administration that was being done—and they must not forget that that counted as well as legislation. Pope said in the old days : For forms of Governm, nt let fools contest, What's best administered is governed bast."

A LANGASTURE WORKER

Cured ,of Biliousness and indigestion by Bile Beans.

Mr. William H. Yates, of 27, Orford-lane, Warring- ton, says :—" I was troubled with pains in my head, and I felt so sick that I couldn't eat. At times I had such severe pains in my sides that I couldn't walk, and when I went to bed I was unable to move about. I was also greatly trpubled with shortness of breath. I didn't know what to do, and my illness reduced me to a very weak state. Sometimes 1 was so faint that I was scarcely able to stick to my work, but I kept at it until I dropped in the street with severe pains in my. side. "The doctors, after trying many remedies in vain, said that nothing would do me any good, and my case was past curing. Than I tried a sample box of Bile Beans, which made me feel a little easier. I continued with Bile Beans, and now lam again well and strong. I can honestly say that Bile Beans have cured me when doctors had quite failed." Even to healthy and vigorous workers the stress and strain of daily toil is almost unendurable in the long, hot days of summer, and to those who are handicapped with a sluggish liver, a weak and sore stomach, poor circulation, or other ailments of the digestive and nutritive system toil becomes long-drawn torture. INFIRMARY EXTENSION SCHEME: Ladies Take Up the Cause.

Warrington ladies on Wednesday decided to take up the'cudgel on behalf of the Infirmary Extension Scheme and as their efforts will extend over a period of twelve months a substantial contribution should result to the funds of the institution, a scheme for the undertaking of the Work was arranged at a very well attended meeting of ladies which was held at the Town Hall. It was fitting that Mrs. Pickmere should preside as it was her hustand who was the first to advocate the need for an Infirmary in Warrington and it was largely as the result of his untiring efforts that the necessary money was raised to erect the premises. Mrs. PICKMERE, in a happy little speech, said she thought that as the men were doing so well for the proposed extension surely the women could do some- thing. She therefore put a letter in the papers, a letter which she was pleased to say was quickly and very nobly responded to, and £lll came in from women. That was very encouraging. (Applause.) She had asked Miss Litton and Mrs. Dakin to help her and they had very kindly consented to do so. The scheme which she had to suggest was the one which they followed on the occasion of the first jubilee of Queen Victoria. Then th 3 town was divided into wards, two responsible ladies were appointed to superintend the work in each ward and they hi turn appointed their collectors and a house to house collection was made. She thought that was the best thing they could do and that this year they should also include the outlying districts from which patients were sent regularly to the Infirmary and which conse- quently derived a benefit from the institution. The women should do something because she considered that they benefitted more than anyone by the operations of the Infirmary. (Hear, hear.) She had had a great many letters promising assistance and money had also been sent. Amongst those donations which she had received were :—Mrs. Milling and family, £25; Mrs. Mclver, £2O; Mrs. Charlton Parr, £5; Misses Smith, £10; Mrs. Fletcher (Whitley), £5; Mrs. Armitage, £10; Mrs. Lightburn, 10s. 6d. (Applause.) If they approved of the scheme which she suggested she thought it desirable that the work of collecting should not begin for a month or six weeks. (Applausaa.) The scheme was approved. Miss BESSIE DUTTON suggested the advisability of collecting by instalments in the poor districts. She was sure that many who would like to subscribe could not do so unless they gave small sums at stated intervals. • Mrs. Pickmere said that this would be done. It was decided to ask the following ladies to take charge of the respective wards: Thelwal and Statham, I Mrs. Williamson, Mrs. H. Hatton and Mrs. Rylands ; Padgate. !qrs. Buckton ; Latchford. Mrs. John Fair- dough and Mrs. Jackson Holmes ; Latchford Without, Mrs. Tunstall, Mrs. Venn and Mrs. Dakin ; Grappenhall, Mrs. Frost; Stockton Heath. Mrs. Henry Greenall and Mrs. W. Richardson ; Moore, Miss Litton ; Penketh and Great Sankey, Miss Tindsley and Miss Clarke •, Whitley, Mrs. Fletcher; Warrington : Town Hall Ward. Mrs. Rowson and Mrs. Reginald Smith ; St. John's Ward, Miss Spinks ; St. Austin's Ward, Mrs. Henry Fairclough and Mrs. J. E. Wright; Fairfield,Miss Litton ; Orford. Mrs. Densham ; Bewsey, Mrs. 3. H. Smethurst; Whitecross, Mrs. Adams ; and Howley Ward, Mrs. John Davies and Mrs. H. Ward. Mrs. PICKMERE said she desired to issue a warning to the townspeople against bogus collectors. The collect- ing books would be printed and she hoped people would see that they were authentic and contained a list of the names ot the lady collectors for the various wards. I Mrs. E. Ancr:r was unanimously appointed hon. treasurer to the collecting committee. Votes of thanks to the Mayor for the use of the Council Cbambers.and to Mrs. Pickmere for presiding,concludqd the meeting.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending a story which I hope you will like. It is called, "Hasty Walter and the Lost Sums," and I hope it will teach a lesson. I will now begin.

HASTY WALTER AND THE LOST SUMS. Walter and Edgar, I am sorry to say, have quarrelled. "You looked over my sums this afternoon," said Walter, "and copied from me. Where are they I If you get the prize, and I don't, I shall tell Mr. Swain that you cheated." "I did not," replied Edgar. "You are quite mistaken. I had done my sums when I looked at yours." They continued to contradict one another, and use angry words, till at last they came to blows.

Edgar was rambling along the river bank in the evening, when Walter, hearing he had gone thither, followed in order to charge him with copying and hiding his sums. So they met, and this sharp quarrel took place. Walter was quite getting the worst of it when, in the struggle, a roll of papers fell out of his pocket. Edgar stooped and picked them up. "Why, here are your sums," he exclaimed, " they have been in your pocket all the time." Walter examined them and was ashamed. "I did not know it," he said. "I am very sorry; I have made a mistake, and was a great deal too hasty. I beg your pardon." The two friends shook hands and became better friends than ever, aild Walter tried not to be so hasty again. Hoping you will enjoy your birthday.—l remain, your loving niece, NELLIE HALSTEAD.

HOW LEO GOT A DRINK." •

A naturalist was walking with Leo, a favo its Newfoundland dog of great size, one fro 4 e.ay, when he observed the animal's repea 6isappointment on putting his head down drink at sundry ice-covered pools. Af'

!ter one a these disapPointmenta, the naturalist broke the ice with his foot for his thirsty companion. The next time it seemed good to the dog to try and drink, instead of waiting for his master to break the ice as be- fore, he set his own huge paw forcibly on the ice, and with a little effort obtained the water for himself.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending you a story which I hope you will print. The title of it. is:—

TEE ROBBERS' CAVE. There was a widower who lived at the sea- side with his two children, whose names were Jack and Florrie. Jack was thirteen years old, and Florrie was eleven. They lived very hap- pily, and often, on summer evenings, they would go on the shore together, looking for sea anemones.

S. ELLIOTT.

It was on one of these occasions', after a lovely summer day, when it was just beginning to grow dusk, that Jack, looking upe suddenly, saw a body of men in the distance. He gazed at them curiously for some time, without calling his sister's attention to them. She had jui3t discovered a very large anemone, and was show- ing it to her brother, when he grasped her arm, cud said "Hush!"

Jack knew perfectly well who they were; he hid heard his father say that a famous baud of smugglers liveo in a cave somewhere about this coast, and nobody could find out wheie the cave was. "These must be the smugglers," thought he. • 'We will watch and see where tuuy go," he said to his sister.

Evidently they had been already seen, for the men were coming in the direction in which the children were. Jack and Florrie made a haity retreat among the rocks, but in their hurry they banged up against a secret spring which was in the rock. Immediately the rock opened. The children were so dumbfounded that they stood still for some time in blank amazement.

However, they entered. No sooner were they lin than the rock closoeil again. " Click!" they were prisoners. At first they did not realize what danger they were in. But, when they founo that they were shut in, they began to be afraid, and still more so when they heard the sound of hurried footsteps outside the rock. Fortunately they saw a door at the end of the passage, whicn they immediately opened. The door opened into a room full af all kinds, of boxes. Jack and Florrie had only time to crouch down among the boxes and hold their breath, for the men were close upon them. Their only ht.pe was that there might be another way out of the cave.

Dear Uncle Ned,—l am sending you a piece of poetry and I would very much like to see it in the paper, if you have a space for it. I must now close, with best wiehes, from your loving nephew,

ALFRED C. PRESCOTT. THE RIVER OF LIFE. The more we live, more brief appear, Our life's succeeding stages, A day to childhood seems a year, And years like passing ages. The gladeome current of our youth, Ere passion yet disorders, Steals lingering like a river smooth,

Along its grassy borders. But as the care-worn cheeks grow wan, ' And sorrow's shafts fly thicker, Ye stars that, measure life to man, Why seem your courses quicker? When joys have lost their bloom and breath, And life itself is, vapid, Why as we reach the Falls of Death, 4W' Feel we its tide more rapid?

===== 0002601_19060721_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

Donation to the Infirmary.

The half-yearly meeting of the Warrington Co- operative Society was held in the Co-operative Hall, Cairo-street, on Monday evening under the presidency of Mr. T. Critchley. There was a good attendance.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
ILLNESS OF THE SECRETARY.

The CHAIRMAN expressed regret that their secretary (Mr. Rathbone) had been unwell for some weeks and was unable to attend the meeting. He trusted that with care and attention Mr. Rathbone would soon be restored to health and take his usual position amongst them. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19060721_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE_ MR. ROBERT COOK.

The CHAIRMAN said that since their last -imeeting they had lost an old friend of co-operation in the person of Mr. Robert Cook who was one of the founders of the movement in Warrington. He had been a member of the committee for upwards of forty years and he (the chairman) believed be was the oldest member on the register at the time of his death. He had taken the deepest interest in the society from its formation. The meeting passed a vote of condolence with the family.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
THE SOCIETY'S PROGRESS.

The CHAIRMAN said that at the'isTi"-Zeeting it was I suggested that the committee should adopt some plan whereby members might be better educated in co-opera- ' tive principles and also that the advisability of extend- ing the tailoring department should be considered. The committee had decided that the present was not a suitable time to have meetings ,of any description indoors but they hoped during the ensuing quarter to formulate a scheme to provide gatherings for i entertainment and instruction n the winter. With regard to the tailoring department, a deputation had been formed to visit several societies with the object of ascertaining all the information they could before taking any definite steps. The committee had pleasure in presenting another very satisfactory balance sheet, showing that the sales had gone up considerably during the quarter. The amount was £40,343, being £1,024 Cs. 2d. in advance of last quarter and £2,309 65.7 d. more than the corresponding quarter of last year. In fact, it was a record quarter and they had every reason to con- gratulate themselves on the splendid progress they were making in Warrington.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
THE REPORT.

The commit tee reported that the sales for the quarter amounted to £40,343 3s, 2d., showing an increase of £1,024 6s. 2d. over the last quarter and £2,309 6s. 7d. over the corresponding quarter of last year. The branch shops in Knutsford-road were opened for business on April 17th, with sales for the first full week amounting to £267 15s. ; the total for the seven weeks was £1,897 Is. Bd. The whole of this amount did not represent. new business, as more than two thirds of the trade was previously done at the Central. No expense had been spared in the equipment of the shops in anticipation of a considerable increase in the trade.

The report was adopted. THE INFIRMARY EXTENSION.

At 'a meeting of the committee a circular was read from the Board of the Infirmary pointing out the con- gested condition of the wards which had necessitated an appeal to the public for funds to enable an extension to be carried out. After full consideration it was resolved that the matter be left to the quarterly meet- ing for decision. The CHAIRMAN said the committee were in favour of a grant of £5O and, if necessity arose, they could con- sider the matter of an additional donation in a year or two. They however, left the question entirely with the members of the society. Mr. W. HAIGH said that although most of the members of the society subscribed to the Infirmary through various sources, he thought it was their duty to do something as co-operators. He himself thought £5O too small for a society like that to give but he would leave the matter with the meeting. A MEMBER remarked that considering that the workingmen contributed very liberally to the Infirmary in church and works collections and in other ways, he thought the committee were making too great a call upon them. . _ _

'The CHAIRMAN said that bearing in mind the support which workingmen extended to the Infirmary the com- mittee thought it would be out of place to suggest a large amount. , Mr. CROWTHER moved that the Society should give £5O. . .

Mr. TROW seconded and said the meeting ought not to be too lavish with money that did not belong to them. The motion was unanimously carried. ELECTION OE COMMITTEE MEN. The meeting was called upon to elect three committee men. The retiring members were Messrs. Williams, Wadsworth, and Hibbert and they were all nominated along with Mr. Rushton. After some discussion a vote was taken and Messrs. Rushton, Hibbert and Wadsworth were elected.

NORTHERN UNION FOOTBALL.

An important meeting of the Lancashire County Union Committee was held at the Grosvenor Hotel Manchester, on Tuesday evening. . . .

The officials for the ensuing 34;ar were appointed as follows :—President, Mr. Robert Coltinge (Rochdale) ; vice-president, Mr. J. Parker (Barrow) ; hon. treasurer, Mr. J. H. Houghton (St. Helens) ; and hon. secretary, Mr. Joseph Platt (Oldham). Mr. H. M. Forrest (Liver- pool City), and Mr. J. Parker (Barrow) were elected to fill the two vacant seats on the committee. It was decided to play next season's Lancashire and Yorkshire match on the ground of the Salford Football Club at Weaste. The draw for the first round of the Lancashire Senior Cup was made as follows :—St. Helens v. Oldham ; Morecambe v. Barrow ; Widnes v. Runcorn • Warring- ton v. Leigh • Salford v. Wigan ; Liverpool City V. Broughton &Inger:, ; Swinton and Rochdale Hornets, byes. To be played on the grounds of the clubs first named on October 13. It was decided to invite applications for a Cup Com- petition to be competed for by Cheshire Clubs on the usual knock-out principle. It was also resolved to endeavour to promote a similar competition for North Lancashire clubs, who are invited to send in applica- tions.

At a meeting of the Management Committee of the Northern Rugby League Mr. Platt was re-elected presi- dent, while the remaining positions were tilled thus : Vice-president, Mr. P. Lister (Bradford) ; hon. treasurer, Mr. J. Nicholl (Halifax) ; and hon. secretary, Mr. J. H. Houghton (St. Helens).

tyant BY THE CLUBMAN. The "Open Road" in Italy.

Two ladies carried out a tour on bicycles in Italy, starting from Florence, equipped with cameras, a picnic tea basket, and their personal luggage carried in light knapsacks. A charmingly written account of their experiences in the current C.T.C. Gazette' concludes as follows: "The jour- ney had not been marked by thrilling incident, but by simple living joys—the joys of the open road.' The sweet taste of freedom and adventure, the joyful feeling at early morning with a new day's journey to accomplish, and a bright day beckoning, the wild elation of spirits when spinning down those winding mountain roads, fringed with tall cypresses, to revel in the sunny plains below, the sense of luxurious ease when stretched at full length beneath the olive trees, the taste of the red Chianti after a parching morning's ride, those little wayside picnics, the sound of the drovers' cries— these are some of the joys of the ' open road' in Italy, and joys 'hot easily to be forgotten."

The Edinburgh Cyclists' Fancy Dress Parade, which took place in June, has resulted in the dis- tribution of over £l5B to various charities in the Scottish capital. The services of 596 collectors were secured, and a sum of £213 odd gathered from the crowds. The expenses of the parade amounted to £55, although the prizes awarded for the best dresses were presented by various donors. The Brst prize winner represented Robinson Crust:* clad in skins, with monkey, parrot, and goat com- plete, accompanied by his faithful aStendanit Frida7

, The Watered Tram Line.

The Company concerned in the lamentable ruts- away tramcar accident at Highgate some weeks ago appears to have furnished a demonstration of the dangers of the watered tram line in a totally unexpected fashion. It seems that the commoi practice of watering the grooved rails had been indulged in on the day of the catastrophe. Many cyclists bad experienced side-slips that very after- noon, and the application of the brakes on the electric car merely converted it into a sledge, sliding by gravity down an incline on rails coated *with a paste lubricant. - . _

On a-Saturday and Sunday at the spot where the accident took place cyclists pass at the rate of more than 1,000 per hour. Every one of these individually is frequently exposed to considerable danger from the excessive watering of the lines, causing side-slip, which may at any moment hurl the incautious rider beneath the wheels of swift travelling traffic. Hitherto the appeals of cyclists for the watering of tram-lines in London to be restrained within reasonable limits have had but little effect. It is to be hoped that this lamentable accident will have the effect of drawing better attention to our reasonable request. In this respect London is far behind many provincial towns this writer has recently visited, where the watering of the tram. rails has been superseded by the use of a wire brush between the wheels, which keeps the rails absolutely clean without watering, and in addition It is impossible for slipping to occur either to ow/Ilia sr g19.7`

Light Wanted at Sankey and Penketh.

7 Sir,—Can you tell me if gas-lamps are looked upon as I being only necessary in the winter-time at Sankey and Penketh ? I have noticed lately that there is absolutely no light at all on the road from the White Lead Works to Penketh village. Some of the nights have been as dark as " a wolf's throat," and residents have a right to expect their local representatives to see that the £lOOOl is &spelled for the benefit of an enlightened (sic) com- munity. The Liverpool-road has long had an unenvi- able reputation at night-time, and at present it is not the sort of place one would care to let ladies traverse in the; inky darkness that prevails. There are gas- lamps, but they remain unlit, merely adding to the dangers of the road for pedestrians. Perhaps there is supposed to be a moon! Our parish Councillors had better realise that we want something better than moonshine from them !—Yours etc., FOOTPAD.

" The New Conscription Scare."

Sir.—Your leader in the issue of July 11th is con- temptible. A paper which talks of treason in con- nection with a most chivalrous general who has served his country for fifty years with a true devotion is doing its level best to aleniate all patriotic people from the Liberal party. Truly if the gospel of liberty and publicity being the one road to national salvation, is going to create a land where gallant deeds and honour for brave men are alike unknown, then indeed is our much vaunted liberty a poor affair,our publicity in need of being muzzled. What do you blame Lord Roberts for? For disclos- ing the weakness of our defences to our enemies! Do you fondly imagine that the German or the French Governments have never read the report of the Norfolk Commission? Do you innocently believe that foreign nations never send emissaries to spy out the land, to report that so little trained are the Volunteers that disciplined foes will mow them down like grass? You call this treason? I will tell you what treason is—to keep silent when you know that by speaking out you may avert a national calamity. You reply : Lord Roberts was Commander-in-Chief, why dicrnot he put the things to rights during his three years of office? The answer is surely obvious; he could do but little because the only solution is universal training (you call it conscription, for party purposes). As long as he was on the active list he was debarred from speaking publicly. Immediately he is free, he comes forward despite his many years, despite his wel known title to rest on his laurels, and tells the people to wake up, rouse themselves and put their house in order before it be too late. _ _ _

You blame him for speaking before Mr. Haldane I disclosed his programme. On the face of it there would seem to be some grounds for your complaint. When however we reflect that from previous utterances it was certain that the war minister was going not only to shirk but to fail to point out the true solution, it is quite evident that the noble Earl was justified, indeed was only acting patriotically in helping to pave the way by speaking out with all the weight of his vast experience in favour of a national army. It would make this letter too long to enter into the question of compulsory -versus voluntary training. It has been argued already in your columns,land I do not doubt that a very large number of your readers are con- vinced that the true system of a citizen force is one where each man does his torn. _

This letter is written to protest against the language in your leader and to show how unjust are the taunts which you hurl at the head of a gallant gentleman.— Yours etc.,

G. R. CROSFIELD.

Volunteer Quarters, July 16th, 1906.

3. That it has never provided the necessary power of expansion in time of war. 4. That it is the most costly and extravagant system in the world, and cannot fail to become more so in spite of temporary reductions. 5. That it is even more costly than appears from the estimates since there is a greater wastage in death, disease and desertion than in any other army in the world. At the present moment the Regulary Army is 17,000, the Militia 37,000, and the Volunteers 102,000 short of I the numbers voted by Parliament. The auxiliary forces ; have been declared by the highest authorities to be i incapable of meeting regular troops and, as the Duke of Norfolk pointed out in the House of Lords in June, I 1904, the breakdown has been due "essentially to the nature of the voluntary system itself, which makes it impossible to demand a reasonable standard of efficiency without greatly reducing the forces." What, we may ask, are the mysterious qualities which I are supposed to enable the British people alone among I the nations of the world to prepare themselves for the business of war on the pleasant, happy-go-lucky lines suggested by Mr. Haldane while other nations have found, as Mr. Arnold Forster pointed out, that they I can only do so by the sacrifice of time, effort, and money. On the other hand, what grounds are there for the statement that "compulsion would be of no avail "to provide the powers of expansion outside the limit of the regular forces? Such assertions, and wild figures as to the cost of military service, may please the groundlings, but they I are directly contradicted by experience, which shows that universal military training is the only system I which, in ancient and modern times alike, has provided powers of expansion and a solid basis of national de- fence for all civilised countries, and that it has done so at a reasonable cost. I Mr. Haldane is no doubt obliged by the exigencies of his position to make one last effort to bolster up a system, which under all fine phrases, hides national selfishness, military inefficiency and the risk of grave disaster. But the historian who records his failure will lassuredly deplore the strange arithmetic and the catch- ! word phrases which prevent the nation from listening to Lord Roberts' and Lord Milner's statemanlike advocacy of the doctrine that a great nation should rely" br its own defence and the defence of its position on its whole manhood and not on a limited professsonal class," and the warning that "in the long run the people who are prepared to undergo the trouble and face the danger of personal service will outstrip not only in war but also in the competitions of peace ' the peoples who fail to realise their responsibilities. , —Yours &c. GEORGE SHEE, secretary. I The National Service League, 72, Victoria st., S.W. I 14th July, 1906.

The Friends' Meeting House and Burying Place at Penketh.

Sir,—On the lovely evening of a lovely day in June I had the privilege of spending a" winged hour" in the precincts of the Friends' Meeting House and Burying Place at Penketh. The westering sun was slowly bringing his accustomed round to a close in a cloudland toned and tinted with his farewell glories, while the Meeting House and the graves around it reflected their lovely glow. It was a peaceful hour in a peace. inspiring spot. The Meeting House is a plain, substantial structure of red bricks, and its walls bear signs that they have borne the sunshine and the storms of many years. The stained glass windows and other devices for ornament which one sees in other places of worship were entirely absent here. Indeed nothing could be more severely plain and simple than the whole exterior of the building. And the same simplicity and lack of show distinguished the iaterior too. As believers in Him who is a spirit and can only be acceptably worshipped in spirit and in truth, the Friends have no need for aids to their devotion in paintings, music, and a "dim religious light." And in beautiful keeping with their humble House is the Friends way of public worship. No bell calls them together ; they have no paid official to say or read prayer for them; no choristers and no soloists. Theirs is a silent wait upon God whose spirit alone can move the soul to true devotion, and inspire the inward breathings for light and guidance which the hearer of prayer will graciously answer if in accordance with His xvill. _

.. _ • Prayer is the sours sincere desire, Uttered or unexpressed; The motion of a hidden fire, Which trembles in the breast.—Montgomery. Looking round on the burying-place now arrayed in all the loveliness of the English sunset hour I thought what an appropriate spot it was for "the sleep which knows no waking until the heavens be no more." Far away from the sins and sorrows of congested and struggling human life, and almost alone with nature, here surely " the wicked cease from troubling and the weary are at rest." And here, too, it was touching to observe that the same simplicity and humility which mark the friends in their life and worship accompanied them in their death also. The desire for effect and the incongruity observable in some other places of sepul- ture are unknown here. Each body has a grave to itself, and the eraves are arranged in rows, each one covered with a tiny mound of earth, with a small stone at the bead recording the birth and death of the sleeper below. Erelong, with kindred sleeping In shades the Saviour blest; Safe in His gracious keeping, Our weary frame shall rest. That sleep shall know no waking Through dreams of pain or care ; Nor strife, not tumult breaking, Disturb the silence there.—Clindinning. Simplicity of life, or worship, and of burial are beautiful and distinguishing features of the Society of Friends, and spring from a desire to follow, as nearly as possible, in the footsteps of our great" Examplar" who was "meek and lowly in heart." Pomp and ceremony, and the assumption of titles in connection with Christianity are altogether inconsist- The Wirral and Birk?nhesd Agricultural Society's ent with the lowly and self-denying nature of the Gospel of Christ. SECONDARY SCHOOL SPORTS.

Successful Festival at Padgate.

The annual sports in connection with the Warrington Secondary School were held on Thursday in the playing field at Padgate. The sports were inaugurated last year and the success which was then achieved was repeated on Thursday. The afternoon, generally, was fine and apprehensions which were occasioned by a shower shortly after the proceedings commenced were quickly dispelled with a burst of glorious sunshine. There was an excellent ring of spectators and great interest was manifested in the various events. Bronze medals were presented to the winners of the respective contests whilst the winning of three races entitled the competitor to a silver medal. At the conclusion of the sports the prizes were distributed by Alderman Bennett amidst cheers. In a preliminary speech the whilst expressing his high appreciation at the efforts of the scholars for physical improvement, hoped they would not lose sight of their more import- ant intellectual studies. _ . .

The Examiner, Saturday, July 21, 1906.

BIG BRIDGE SCHEME. Three New Bridges--Suggested in the Borough.

We understand that there is before the Town Council a scheme for providing three bridges over I the Mersey. It is suggested that the bridges should be built by the new ferro-concrete process, and the deputation which was recently appointed to visit other towns where bridges have been so constructed expressed its entire approval of the method. It is proposed that one bridge should be placed across the Mersey near the Stag Bridge at Walton to join up with a new road which, under an agreement I with the Corporation, the owners have undertaken Ito make across Arpley meadows, to terminate at the new bridge over the railway near Bank Quay station. Another proposal is that a new bridge should I be constructed across the Mersey at Howley, with a road leading to it through Victoria Park and also, that instead of altering Warrington Bridge by means of cantilevers, a new 60 feet Bridge should be substituted.. We are informed that it is estimated that the three bridges could be con- structed for less than the estimated cost of constructing the proposed iron bridge, the plan of which was prepared by Mr. J.J. Webster some time ago. The advantage of the ferro-concrete ' system is that the bridges require no annual maintenance, whereas an iron bridge would take at i least £3OO to maintain. Each bridge would have a single span across the river and would not I only give greater headway under the bridge but would lower the crown of the road over it. Bridges of the type suggested are very rapidly con- structed and any one of the three could be put up within three months. The great advantage of a bridge across the river at Walton would be the opening up of Arpley meadows —3OO acres of land I practically in the centre of the town which could be developed for building purposes. At present the hind is of little use owing to the lack of access to it. We understand that a report on the subject will be presented in a short time.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
ST. MARY'S CHURCH TOWER. --- Gifts of Bells Completed.

Townspeople passing St. Mary's Church, Butter market-street, will notice that the erection of the hand- some new tower is progressing rapidly: When com- pleted the tower which is, of course, in the Gothic style of architecture in karmony with the Church itself will be a graceful ornament to a fine building. It has been designed by Mr. Pugin Powell, the well known Liverpool architect, and the building of it has been entrusted to Messrs. Travis and Wevill, Limited, of Liverpool. For about two months the workmen have been busily engaged on the improvement and it is anticipated that the work will be finished in time to allow of the holding of a special dedication service in the earlier part of October. The tower will contain a splendid peal of bells, and the fall number of eight has been subscribed. We are able to give the list of donors as follows :—£l3o bell, a benefactor; £7O bell, a benefactor ; £5l bell will be subscribed locally as a memorial to the late Father Sumner, a former Rector of the Church ; £5O bell, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P. ; £l3 bell, Mr. R. Shepherd ; £37, a benefactor ; £33 bell, Mr. John Richardson and family ; £3O bell, Mr. and Mrs. Henry Houghton. The bells will be ready for placing in the tower towards the end of September.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0130.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. JOHN BURNS AND THE UNEMPLOYED.

Government Grant of £200,000.

MORE WORK UNDER BETTER CONDITIONS.

Mr. John Burns, in the House of Commons yester- day (Thursday \ made the announcement that the Government will give a grant of £200.000, to dis- tress committees next winter to help the workless people. Mr. Burns was speaking on the vote for the Local Government Board on the working of the Unemployed Workmen's Act. His proposals for dealing with the unemployed and the programme of social legislation which he adumbrated met with a large measure of support from the Labour party. His plans in brief are as follows : Two hundred thousand pounds to meet distress next winter to be distributed to Distress Committees. Bill for rural housing, similar to the Irish Labourers Bill. Amendments of the laws dealing with small holdings and allotments. Amendment of the Crofters Acts. Amelioration of the conditions of work of dock labourers. Employment to be found for ex-army, navy, and militia men, Emigration for vagrants.

NOTES ON CAMES AND ATHLETICS.

(Continued front Page G.)

Cycling. Saturday was a capital day for cycling awl about fourteen members of the Warrington Club turned out for the run to Sandbach. The outing was thoroughly enjoyed, several members staying for the week-end. On Tuesday evening Glazehury was the destination and a pleasant time was spent in playing at howls on the green attached to the Raven Hotel.

Swimming. Warrington have succeeded in taking three points out of South Hill in the Liverpool and District League In the match at Warrington the game ended in a draw whilst on Wednesday Warrington journeyed to the South Hill baths and won by seven goals to five . Ashton was the principal scorer and the majority of Warring. tons goals were placed to his credit.

On Thursday Warrington took a weak team to Burslem and Were beaten by two goals to one. At half time there was no score though \Varrington were hay- ing the best of the argument. In the second half they were however handicapped by the absence of Woolsten- croft who had to leave the baths through cramp, and it was largely through this that they were • beaten.

A junior handicap for a prize offered by Messrs. Tanner Bros., Tanners-lane, was decided in the War- rington baths the first place being secured by P. Chalmers.

The next match will be played at the Baths on Thursday evening, when St. Helens will be the visitors. The result should be a win for Warrington. The second ronnd of the Works Squadron Competition will also be swum aft the same evening. when the following teams will compete :—Crosfields No. 2 v. Corporation, Monks Hall v. Richmonds, Crosfields No.l v. Pearson and Knowles, Fletchers a bye. The meeting of Crosflelds No. v. Pearson and Knowles should provide some good swimming as these teams ore looked upon as the winners of the Competition.

, Local Sheplierdry The Warrington District of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds A.U. held its quarterly meeting at the Shepherds by Night Lodge, Temperance Hotel, Lymm, on Saturdqy. Bro. Joseph Mather Prov. Chief Shepherd presided. He was supported by Deputy Prov. Chief Shepherd T. Baskerville, Dis, Treas. Bro. James Parr, Dis. Sec. Bro. Peter Edgar, Dis. Trustees Bros. W. Eckersley and W. Hall, and the Delegates representing the Lodges in the District. The meeting having been duly opened the District Sec. read the accounts for the past quarter, which showed that the funeral claims had been exceptionally heavy. On the whole however, the various funds of the District were in a satisfactory condition and on the auditors (Bros. J. Gresty and T. Miller) having certified their correct- ness the accounts were passed. After the passing of new members the meeting then proceeded to the election of district officers for the year 1900-7. Bro. Baskerville was unanimously elected to the position of Prov. Chief Shepherd. For the office of Deputy Prov. Chief Shepherd there were four candidates nominated, and after a somewhat interesting fight Bro. , Peter L. Edgar was elected to the position. ' During the meeting, P.P.C.S. Bro. Ashton submitted an interesting account of the proceedings of the A.M.C. held at Pontypridd in Whit week, giving an epitome of some of the chief subjects that were dis- cussed. The report was well received by the Brethren and after several members, including Bros. Weir, ! Haley, Mather, Hall, and Parr had favourably com- mented upon it, Bro. Ashton was heartily thanked for ! his services at the A.M.C.—After the installing of the newly-elected officers, the meeting passed a hearty vote of thanks to Bro. J. Mather for his valuable ser- vices during his term of office as Pro. Chief Shepherd
