THE CYCLE PARADE.

FULL PROGRAMME.

Warrington has witnessed many picturesque proces- sions, but the Fancy Dress Parade and Carnival which will be held to-day (Saturday), on behalf of the Infirmary Extension fund promises to eclipse all previous efforts of the kind. Knowing that they are working in a truly noble cause, the promoters have given unsparingly of their time and ability to ensure the complete success of the parade. The Cycling Club, with their usual zeal for such worthy projects, took the lead and have been admirably backed up by a large and thoroughly representative band of workers from other organizations. Every detail has been carefully attended to and the arrangements could not be better. It is confidently affirmed that only fine ' weather is needed to make the parade a record one for Warrington. All taking part in the procession must assemble round the Band-stand in Bank Park by 4.30 and the procession will start promptly at 5 o'clock and traverse the following route : —Sankey-street, Butter- market, Church-street, round the lamp opposite the Parish Church,Church-st., Mersey-st., Bridge-st.,Horse- market-street, Tanners-lane, Froghall-lane, Crosfield- street, Parker-street, Wilson-Patten-street, past the canons, Bridge-street, Rylands-street and Palmyra- square to the Parr Hall. To while away the tedium of waiting a Perrot troupe (consisting of local artistes) will give performances opposite Miss Garven's house, Sankey-street, from three to four o'clock and at Bridge Foot (Mersey-street end) from five to six o'clock. Mr. 11. Ashton is acting as chief marshal of the pro- cession with Mr. E. Morris as hon. secretary of the Marshals' Committee. A motor car will announce the approach of the procession which will be in the following order : SECTION No. 1. Detachment of Police. Ist V.B. South Lancashire Regiment Band. Messrs. MEETING OF GENERAL COMMITTEE.

The weekly meeting of the General Committee was held at the Patten Arms Hotel on Friday evening. Mr. W. Peter Rylands presided and was heartily welcomed by Mr. Pickavance, on behalf of the committee. Mr. RYLANDS said that, although he had not been present at previous meetings, the chief motive he had in keep- ing away, was that he felt they were doing so well, and that the committee would robably prefer to work without him. Mr. Hampson had kept him closely informed as to what they were doing. He appreciated most warmly the enthusiasm and good work which was being carried out, and he looked forward to a tremendous success on Saturday. (Applause.) He hoped to join with them in receiving the congratulations which he was sure the committee would receive on their success. (Applause.) Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. intimated that their brigade would take part in the procession, and the Runcorn Cycling Club sent word that about 25 of their members would be in attendance in costume. _

A letter was read from the Mayor, intimating that the same privileges as on previous occasions would be granted—to meet in the Park and use the rooms at the Town Hell for the distribution of boxes, &c. Permission has also been granted for the use of the Drill Hall. Mr. Witrrnam, on behalf of the Prize Committee, stated that about £2O 15s. had been collected. It was decided to give the prize for the fire brigade in money. Mr. DAVENPORT, for the Entertainment Committee, said it had been decided, in case of unfavourable weather, to give the Pierrot entertainment in the Parr Hall. Admission would be free with a charge of 6d. for the plush seats. Mr. G. H. Smug, for the Novelty Committee, said the brick competition had been a great success. He urged the desirability of returning the books at an early date. It was decided to ask Lady Grcenall to distribute the prizes at the coacert. LATEST ARRANGEMENTS.

The Ganeral Committee met again on Wednesday evening. Mr. %V. H. BELLTAN presided and said they were nearing the consummation of their efforts and, given fine weather, he had no doubt that the parade would be a splendid success. The meetings of the General Committee would be continued on the Friday evenings after the parade until all the business had been disposed of, and he hoped the enthusiasm would be maintained.

A letter was read from Mr. W. P. Rylands regretting his inability to attend and stating that his services would be at the disposal of the committee on Saturday. Mr. Peter Knight wrote stating that owing to Maass in his family it might be impossible for him to arrange for the Anglers' Association exhibit to appear in the parade. It was decided to send a letter of sympathy to Mr. Knight. Mr. WHITHAM, on behalf of the Prize Committee, said a splendid selection of prizes had been purchased and they were new on view in Messrs. Boydell Bros.' window. Mr. BAINES for the Novelty Committee, said the watch competition had made great progress this week Mr. PRITCHARD, for the Collector' Committee reported that arrangements had been made for nine collecting lurries and about 300 collectors had promised their services. It was stated that members of the local Ambulance Corps would be on street duty at various points of the route in case of accident. Various matter of detail were discussed and it was evident that the most complete arrangements had been made to ensure the success of the parade.

DEATH AT HIS POST.

James Meadows, aged fifty-one, of Phythian-street, St. Helens, watchman at Messrs. Pilkington's timber works, was found dead on Saturday morning shortly before six o'clock. Meadows had occupied this post for a good many years,and was a well respected servant of the company. He had attended to his duties as watchman during the night in the usual way, but appeared te have been seized with illness, and when the other watchman came on at six o'clock he found him dead.

===== 0002601_19060721_art0136.txt | included=true | included =====
A SUICIDE'S WILL.

Late Mr. C. Higginbottom Leaves All to

His Sweetheart.

THE WILL CONTESTED BY HIS MOTHER.

[SPECIA.L REPORT.]

In the Probate Division, on Tuesday, the President (Sir Gorrell Barnes) and a special jury commenced the hearing of the case of Turnock v. Higginbottom. The plaintiff was Miss Henrietta Tunstall Turnock, of Wttitefield-road, Stockton Heath, and she sought probate of a will dated Bth November, 1905, made by Mr. Charles Higginbottom for many years secretary to Arthur Waring and Company, of Warrington. The testator committed suicide a few days after proposing marriage to Miss Turnock. The will in dispute bequeathscl the whole of his property, valued at about £2,500 to her. The defendant was Mrs. Sarah Higgin- bottom, the mother of the testator, who opposed the grant on the ground that her son was not of sound mind at the time the document was executed, and that he did not know and approve of its contents. She set up a prior will dated the 21st July, 1905, under which she was to have an allowance of .£1 a week.

Mr. Barnard, IC.C., and Sir. Pritchard appeared for the plaintiff, and Mr. Norman Craig represented the defendant. Mr. Barnard, K.C., in opening said that the testator was a man about 55 years of age at the date of his death on the 11th November 1905, three days after the execution of the witl now in dispute. The testator entered the firm of Messrs. Arthur Waring and Co., of Warrington in 1871 as a junior clerk and from that date down to the time of his death he remained in the firm's service. He had raised himself from junior clerk to secretary to the company. In 1882 he married lady who unfortunately died in 1903. She went to Harrogate with her husband and died suddenly in the street. After the death of his wife, testator's mother came to stay at his house and she remained with him down to the time of his death. The plaintiff in this action, Miss Turnock, was a lady about 30 years of age who had been a friend of the testator and his late wife some seven years and in 1905 testator paid her a great deal of attention and on November Ist of that year he proposed marriage to her. The lady required some few days to consider the matter and on the 3rd November she accepted the offer. On the 4th Novem- ber Miss Turnock took the testator to her mother's house in Whitefield-road, Stockton Heath and introduced him to her family. On the sth November testator took plaintiff and introduced her to his mother as her future daughter-in-law and on the oth he handed plaintiff an envelope and asked her to read it. They were out walking at:the time and Miss Turnock was not to read the document until she got home. He went home with her and she then opened the envelope and read the document prepared by the testator in which he left all his property to her. After she had read it the testator asked Mrs. Wilson, who had employed plaintiff as com- panion, to witness it and Mrs. Wilson witnessed it. That, however, was not the will in question. There was an arrangement between the testator and the plaintiff that she should go to his house on the 9th November. She went there and saw Mr. Jenkins a solicitor who had come to see the testator. Mr. Jenkins had known the testator for some 30 years and whenever there was any legal business to be done Mr. Jenkins had always acted for the,testator. Mr. Jenkins had prepared two wills before the one now in question. Mr. Higginbottom introduced Miss Turnock to Mr. Jenkins and then produced the paper and asked Mr. Jenkins whether it was a good will. Mr. Jenkins told him it was not. The testator then re- quested Mr. Jenkins to draw up a proper one. It was accordingly arranged that Mr. Jenkins and Mr. Higginbottom should meet next day for the purpose of making a new will. On the Bth, accordingly Mr. Jenkin's drew up a new will by which the testator left his property to Miss Turnock, Mr. Jenkins pointed out to the testator that it was rather severe not leaving the property to his mother who under the early will was to receive £1 per week for life. The testator then ex- pressed the wish to make some provision for his mother and an agreement was drawn up to that effect which was subsequently signed by Miss Turnock. The will having been drawn up it was signed in the presence of Mr. Jenkins and his clerk who were the attesting witnesses. The same day the plaintiff met testator in Warrington and on the way home from Stockton Heath be told her that he had made his will and that ha had made it in her favour. On the 9th the testator came to see the plaintiff and brought her an 'engagement ring. On the 10th they met again and dis- cussed the wedding which they proposed should take place shortly. They parted on perfectly affectionate terms and an appointment was made for the next day. On the 11th the plaintiff went to meet the testator but he did not turn up and she never saw him alive again. It appeared that he went to his office at the usual time. He was perfectly capable and spoke to people in his office about his engagement. He afterwards went home and seemed to have gone into an outhouse and com- mitted suicide. Miss Turnock was called and gave evidence in support of her' case. She said she lived at Whitefield-road, Stockton Heath, and was 32 years of age. She described Iher acquaintance with the testator and said that during the whole of the time she had known him he had always seemed cheerful and had shown no signs of depression. She first heard of his death on a Sunday evening when she was handed a letter in his handwriting. It was in these terms

" My dear darling Hettie,—May you and God forgive me for the rash act. I cannot bear it longer and in this state of health it is simply dreadful. Take full advan- tage of my last will and don't let my brother have a single penny. I give all to you for your love and kind- ness to MO.—CHARLES HIGGINROTTOM."

" P.S.—You will find £l5 in my safe drawer at the office. God bless you dear and look after poor old mother." Cross-examined by Mr. Norman Craig, plaintiff said she had never been to the testator's house during his late wife's lifetime. She was not aware that his wife's death affected him very much though she knew he was much upset because it was so sudden. Was not the marriage with you to take place shortly? —Yes.

Did ihe questions of the will ariss at all 2—No. You had no discussion with him about it ?—No. Did you know thut at this time he WM being seen by three doctors ?—No.

_ Did he talk to you about himself 'stall ?—tiery little. Did he say he was suffering from insomina ?—No. He never complained of sleeplessness ?—No. Mrs. Higginbottom was an old lady about 80 years of age ?—Yes. Was not she left entirely without means ?—I did not know at the time but I understand so since. Did you regard the agreement you signed agreeing to provide for Mrs. Higginbotom binding upon you ?—f did.

Were you aware that gas and water were cut off at the house ?—No. - _ _

William Henry J. Jenkins,solicitor.of Warrington said he had known the testator about 30 years and was a friend of his.

His lordship what is the value of the property? Witness : About £2,500. Witness said that he had drawn up the other will for the,testator under which the mother was to have £i per week. Mr. Barnard : Did he ever complain of his health ? Witness : Ho complained but I did not think it was anything serious. Had you the slightest suspicion there was anything wrong with testator's mind at that time ?—No. It was suggested at thefinquest on the testator that you had a scene 'pith Miss Turnock and you told her that unless you acted for her she would not receive .£l. Was there any truth in that ?—No ; not the slightest foundation.

What did take piece then ?-4 had been very busily engaged and Miss Turnock said she had been advised to instruct someone to appear at the inquest on her behalf. That was entirely contrary to my view as I thought there was no necessity. Mr. Norman Craig (cross-examining) : He com- plained to you of pains in the head? Witness : No; I co:nplained to him of pains in my head. (Laughter.) Mr. Craig : We will not deal with your pains at present. (Renewed laughter.) Mr. Norman Craig, in opening the defence, said his lease was that the wife's tragic death seriously affected the testator's mind. There was a crowd in the street one day, and thinking it was an accident he took no notice of it, but went on to his office. He afterwards heard that A woman had been taken to the hospital. Subsequently be was informed that the lady was his wife, end that she had died in the street. After this he was seldom well and suffered from melancholia. The health of the testator imOctober and November, 1905, was far from being good, and as a fact it was as had as it could be. He consulted two other doctors besides his own, and they could tell the jury that he suffered from insomnia. The testator was in a desolate condition and complained of insomnia, nervous debiliiy and inability to take solid food, and his health was in such a con- dition that marriage was out of the question. This was in October and November and then they got the culminating act of November 11th when the man din- troyed himself. On November 7th and Bth the testator was in anything but a proper frame of mind. He hoped that after the jury had heard the evidence they would cometo the conclusion that the testator could not truly appreciate the facts of the case and that his evidence would enable them to hold that his testamentary capacity was not intact. On November Bth the testator was in the hands of three doctors, two of whom would describe his condition as nervous incoherent and rambling. He was about to commit himself to a proposition of marriage which his health did not justify his undertaking. On November Bth the testator came to the conclusion that as a last resort he had made a contract of marriage with a young lady, against whom he had not a word to say, which be felt unable to carry out because of his nervous condition. Having done this arid beies unable to carry it out then he went and made the will in question. He had an old mother aged 80 and she had given up her home and had gone to live with him as she was practically penniless if this

suit went against her. He asked the jury to consider this matter seriously, bearing in mind the fact that the son had apparently ignored all sense of filial objection to his mother.

Mrs. Sarah Higginbottom was then called and examined by Mr. Norman Craig. How old was your son at the time of his desth 9-54. He was married in January 1833 to Miss Hamilton ? Yes.

And she died in 1903 ?—Yes. Have you heard stated in Court the circumstances of her death and is that true ?—Yes. Up to that time you had lived separately in a different house about 11 minutes' walk away ?—Yeg. After his wife's death did you go and live with him ? Yes.

Before his wife's death, of what sort of disposition was your son ?—Very nice disposition. Very cheerful. Was he a temperate man ?—Very. What was the effect of his wife's dtath upon him ? It made him very melancholy. Before his wife's death did he entertain his friends a greal deal ?—Not very much. After his wife's death were there photographs of his wife everywhere ?—Yes they were all over the house, in every room. In October and November 1905 was there a change in his health ?—Yes, he became very nervous and debilitated.

Did he suffer for want of sleep ?—Yes. After his first wife died did he give way to drink ? Yes,he gave way to whisky and drank heavily. Did you see Miss Turnock after your son's death ? Yes at the inquest on my son, and she then said she had only known my son a fortnight. What happened on Sunday?-0n Sunday morning I found his bed had not been slept in, and in the evening I inquired of his friends.—On the Monday his body was found. During the last fortnight of his life did you notice any marked changed in him 7—Very much so. He was not himself at all.

As regards yourself have you always been on affec- tionate terms with your son ?—Always. I have never had a cross word with him.

Cross-examined by Mr. Barnard: His wife's death upset him very much ?—Yes. And it was some time before he got over the shock?— Yes, it was quite six months after. When did he first inform you of his engagement with Miss Turnock ?—lt was on November 5. You took his tea up to him in bed ?—Yes. Did he say" there is a lady in my tea," alluding to a piece of tea leaf floating on the tea?—No he said there was a lady in the case. I asked about her and he told me who she was and that he had been to see her mother, I and that he was going' to introduce her to me. She came and was introduced to you ?—Yes. Did you tell Miss Turnock there was anything the matter with your son's mind ?—Oh, no. Dr. Joseph was his regular doctor was he not ?—Yes. Mrs. Eliza Margaret Bates, the wife of Mr. Edwin Bates, gave evidence that latterly she had often seen the testator worse for drink. Dr. S. J. Parker stated that the testator lived next door to him for about 10 years. He attended him in 1904 for insomnia and nervous depression. in January, March and April of 1905 he also attended the testator for the same complaint. in October and November he was much worse than he had been. Ho became so excited that witness became very anxious about him, and then he was in a terrible state of depression, and he was not in such a fit state as to transact such important business as the making of a will. Cross-examined : The testator would be ranch agitated as well as depressed. Witness gave him draughts with the idea of allaying the insomnia and steadying his condition. At this point the further hearing was adjourned. The hearing was resumed on Wednesday. Dr. Charles Ernest Richmond giving evidence for the defence said that in October last he made a thorough examination of Mr. Higginbottom. He found nothing organically wrong with him but his mind was unbalanced and he was quite unfit for work. He was taciturn in his manner and could not sleep nor eat. In examination by Mr. Barnard K.C. witness said that his opinion of the capacity of the testator was not affected at all by the fact that he wrote a letter just before he committed suicide. He formed the opinion that the answers the testator gave to the ques- tions put to him though apparently straightforward were not sensible.

Were they as sensible as the answers yon are giving now in the box ?-4 don't intend to flatter either of us. (Laughter). Charles William Davenport. builder, of Stockton Heath, said he had known the testator for '2O years. Since the death of Mrs. Higginbottom, his wife, in 1903 he had not been the same roan. He had suffered from fits of depression and about a month before his death those fits had become more noticeable. He remembered that when he was walking home with the testator on November Bth, the latter, who had been very quiet sAid " I feel-- bad." They went into the "Red Lion "for a drink and testator said" I have done it. I have signed a document." Witness told him that if it was »will or a marriage settlement he had better be careful about it. The testator went _

he had known but had not sok He added "I am not fit to marry anyone!' Though a very temperate man the testator would have at times— not exactly a burst but something next door to it." (Laughter.) _

Fr„ederick Howard, a neighbour, also gave evidence of the depressing effects which his wife's death had on the testator. On November sth testator told him he had made "a —fool " of himself. He proposed to a lady and had been accepted. He told witness that ho MA in very bad health and had had to take medical advice. Dr. Dreshfleld had advised him to get more cheerful company saying that his mental condition was not really bad. Dr. Maurice Craig, mental specialist, said that a hypochondriaclwould not commit suicideand no man committed suicide without some cause of a definite nature such as financial trouble. The President : But there were in the old Roman days noblemen who when they got tired of life got into a bath and opened a vein. Dr. Craig: That NVB,9 a custom. He was probably obliged to do it. Mr. Norman Craig mentioned kari-kari and methods of suicide adopted sometimes in continental armies. Dr. Craig : That was a custom again—mere custom, an undesirable custom perhaps, but a custom. In cross-examination witness said he had heard the evidence in this case and it was perfectly possible for the testator to have conducted his business in a proper manner and still be suffering from mental depression and morbid fears.

This concluded the evidence and Counsel proceeded to address the jury. The President (Sir Gorell Barnes) in the course of his summing op remarked that the facts were abort and the issues definite in the case. Whether the will was n reasonable one or not was not a question which the jury would have to consider. They could not, of course, and must not shut out from their minds the fact that the man committed suicide, but they would have to look at all the facts which were before them. There was no doubt that this man was not in good health but he conducted his business and seemed to have been competent to give instructions with regard to the making of the will. It was said, however, that the man had reached such a stage that he might conceivably have been inclined to act on a sudden impulse without properly considering and weighing what it was he was doing..

- The jury retired to consider their verdict and found that the will was duly executed and that the deceased was of sound mini. They also expressed the hope that the agreement with regard to qrs. Higginbottom would be held as part and parcel of the will. Mr. Barnard K.C. said that the agreement would be carried out. The President then pronounced for the will.

The Liverpool and District Mineral Water Manufacturers and Bottlers' Association do hereby give PUBLIC NOTICE and WARNING to householders and consumers of aerated and non-intoxicating Beverages that it is unlawful to pur- chase, sell, use, detain or destroy Mineral Water Bottles, Boxes and Syphons, bearing the name or trademark of the owners. PADGATE. _— DEATH OF MRS. SLATER.—Much sympathy will go out to Mr. Slater. the manager of Mr. Farrington's Farm, Fearnhead Cross, on the loss of his wife who died last Friday night and was buried on Monday in Pad gate Churchyard. Mr. and Mrs. Slater had been married npwards. of 47 years and Mr. Slater is sure to feel her loss deeply after so long an association. -4s RETIREMENT OF INSPECTOR EGERTON, OF RUNCORN. The inhabitants of Runcorn will learn with regret of the impending retirement of Inspector Egerton after 2,5} years service in the Cheshire Constabulary. His first few years in the force were spent at Altrincham, under Major Little (then captain), now Chief Constable of Preston, and afterwards he was in the Wallasey and West Kirby districts, where he was promoted to sergeant. Subsequently he was five years at Over, whence he was transferred to Runcorn, with the rank of inspector, and the magistrates and inhabitants of Over recorded their appreciation of his services in a testi- monial and the presentation to him of a handsome gold watch and a purse of gold.

•*rl'-o," said Gretton, who Was not Impeccable, "I suppose not." "And, besides my pay, I have four hundred a year." "Cigars," grunted Gretton. No, I smoke a pipe, and my tobacco does not cost much. And now lam in very, good odour at Simla." "When were you fumigated?" asked Gretton, with half a smile. "For I know a young lady who used to say how badly you were treated." Richard wondered how much Madge's father knew. "Things have only altered for the better with me lately. But before I left India I had a long talk with Sir Eustace Camborne' and he was very com- plimentary. I don't think there can be any doubt of my getting on now." "But all the fame it's a very bad marriage for my daughter," said Gretton; "and even if I was brouga—gradually--to think of the matter, I doubt very much whether Mrs. Gretton would consent." "Oh, sir!" cried Richard. "But with your help that would be got over in time." "I don't know so much about that,". said Gretton, dieconsolately. He was really beginning to take sides -With Blundell. - "I think you will find your son likes me," said Richard, after a little pause. "Oh, he does, I know that," replied Mr. Gretton, waking out of an arduous imaginary conversatioa with Amelia. "If I'm a judge of men—and I think I you'll have have reason to be proud of him," said hichard. "He's as keen as mustard, and as cool as a cucumber. We had some very hot work with Colonel Briggs." "So I gathered," said the father. "And did Billy distinguish' himself ? " , Richard described the fight where Billy lost his finger and judiciously made the young subaltern the hero of the melodrama. Mr. Gretton heard the , very crack of the guns, and saw Billy walking to and fro; the Sikhs and Goorkhas climbed the ; mullah's inaccessible path before his eves. "by Jove! I wisn you coma come ano tell tam 1 mother, Blundell," cried Grettou, who was greatly excited. I II will, sir." "Then we must arrange it," said Gretton, hastily. "I'll let you know. But, Captain Blundell, nothing I've said must lm taken as a consmt on my part. I must, of course, make inquiries." "If I can help you in any way. I shall be glad," said Richard, who knew when a victory was won, and when it was inadvisable to pursue. He looked on Mrs. Gretton as a dangerous defile to get troops entangled in. That there were friends in her fastnesses was enough. "By Jove! he's a fine fellow. Hang me! He's a very fine fellow," said Gretion. " Upon my soul, I'm not surprised at Madge liking him. I Wish to ' Heavens I'd been in the army myself. If Amelia doesn't like it, she must lump it." But this wiis after lunch. "I've seen him, Madge," he whispered, as they went in to dinner. "Oh!" said Madge, but before she could ask a single question her mother sailed in, and sat down with a rare and satisfied smile. "You wouldn't look so pleased if you knew," thought Gretton. "Are you better to-night, Amelia?" "No, I am not better," cried Amelia, "but I feel better able to bear it." Madge looked brilliant and beaming. "You wouldn't look so pleased if you knew," thought her mother as she ate her way through dinner as if it were an obstacle race. So as all of them thought their own thoughts, and two could not speak before the other, dinner was a silent function. "Oh, did you ? " asked Madge, in the smoking room, when Mrs. Gretton took a nap on the drawing-room sofa. "I did." "And—you liked him?" "What, like the man, who wants to take you away? I hated him." "And do you hate him now, dad? " "Not quite so much. But, Madge, I'm afraid it's not much of a match for you." "Oh, now you talk like mother, dad. \lf-mild you rather have me marry a duke and cry my eye out, or marry a crossing-sweeper and be happy? "Why not marry a duke and be happy ?" "They're all too late, dear dad. They've lost their chance now, poor things. Put Debrett in mourning. But did you like him ?" Mr. Gretton lighted a cigar. "He's not bad, I thought." 011, you dear!" She sat on his knee. "Go away, Madge, you don't want me.. She laid her head on his shoulder. "I won't go." "You will. Yes, I didn't dislike him. But he's very old." "Oh,, ' cried Madge, indignantly, "how can you say such things? Why, you're as young as young can be, and he's much, much yoimger than you." "Well, we won't argue that point," said the father. "But he's very poor." "I shall be rich some day." "And I feel he has a deuce of a temper." "Yes, doesn't he look like it," mused Madge, with evident satisfaction. "I can imagine him just frightening me to death." "And you like the notion?" "I adore it." • "Do I understand you Made?" Madge laughed. "The very idea! Of course not, did. But what's goinl to be done?" "Yes, what ?" "D4d, I think we'd better get married, and tell mother afterwards." "And what about me ?" "You can come to India with us for a trip." "I was thinking of it." Madge smiled, and purred on his shoulder again. "Why, I knew you were. But what about my little plan ? " Mr. Gretton shook his head. "I don't like it. No, it wouldn't, be suitable /I "For a girl and a Gretton, as mother says." He nodded. "You're very young, and can wait a bit, Madge." "But Richard isn't so very young. Oh, yes, he is; but not young enouah to wait very long. And besides, he must go back in six months." "Six months? Impossible, Madge!" "It's a—very—long—time," urged Madge. If it wasn't, I couldn't think of it." "Why, it's no time at all! It's to-morraw 1" "Dad, how absurd of you to say such things I Why, six months are years You know they, are. At any rate, they're lots of time to think in." She combed his hair with her hand, and pulled his ear. "I don't think I love you so much, after all," said her father. "Stuff !" cried Madge. And, going to bed soon after, she felt that all the worst was over. To have one final and awful row with her devoted mother did not seem so terrible. Had not all the long years been passed in preparation for such a battle?

: Thelma] Stationmasters' Retirement. I After 33 years service as stationmaster at Thelwall !Mr. Jacob Kite has retired on superannuation. During ! his residence in the village Mr. Kite has made him- self extremely popular and on Monday evening a I fitting tribute was paid to the respect in which he is• held. For this purpose a meeting was beld in the I , ~,.. : 1 se,_uooo4, the Vie3r presiding over a large attendance. I The chairman in a brief address spoke in the highest 1 terms of Mr. Kite's geniality and of the respect in which he was held in the village. Similar testimony Iwas added by Mr. H. Roebuck, Mr. Taylor, Admiral Jones-Parry, Mr. G. R. Williamson, Mr. J. P. Rylands land Mr. Hatton.—Mrs. Williamson, asked Mr. Kite Ito accept a roll tvith names of 87 subscribers, a very I handsome chiming clock, bearing the following 1 inscription on a silver plate,:—" Presented 29 Mr. IJacob Kite by his friends and neighbours, as a mark I of esteem and respect on the occasion of his retirement . from the position after 33 years as stationmaster of : Thelwall," and a cheque for £74 ss.—ln acknowledg- ing the gifts, Mr. Kite said he had simply done his I duty. He was sorry to sever his connection with ; them as stationmaster, but was glad ta know that i through the kindness of the Admiral it would nOt be ! necessary for him to leave the village wl.ere he had i spent so many happy year.—Mr. H. Kite having I spoken, the proceedings closed with the singing of the National Anthem and "For he's a jolly good fellow." ' A FALLEN SOLICITOR. . William Henry Somerset Cells, *ho described him. I 'self as a solicitor, appeared in the dock at the Warring- son Police Court on Tuesday, to answer a charge of being drunk and incapable in Dolman's-lane the pre- vious night. A police officer found him stretehed out 'across the footpath.—lt was stated by Superintendent I Monaghan that he had forsaken his profesion, end now went about gardening.—A fine of Bs. including Costs, was imposed.--Prisoner asked that he might be allowed to send ertelegram to his friends in order to get the money.

UONTMERCIAL FAlLURES.—According to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," thb total number of Commercial Failures recorded in England and Wales during the week ending Friday, July 13th, was 141, viz. :—New Bankruptcy Proceedings published in the London Gazetke, 72; Deeds of Arrangement registered,69. The respedive numbers in the corresponding week of last year were : vankruptcies, 86 ; Deeds of Arrangement 71—total 157'; being a decrease of IG. The total num- ber of Commercial Failures recorded during the 28 weeks of the present year ix 4,611; the total number recorded in the corresponding 23 weeks of last year was 4,884, showing a decrease of 273.

riew Rotor Dangers continue to be discovered. Some affect the users of motors, like the liability of parts to fracture from crystalliiation of the steel induced by excessive vibration, which has caused several recent accidents. Others are of serious consequence to the general public, like the dangers revealed in Professor Dewar's analysis of the poisonous fumes exhaled by the motor. The number of motor acci- dents seems to be steadily increasing, and at the same time some of the organized bodies repre- senting motorists are distinguishing themselves by the dissemination of wholesale attacks upon the police in districts where the police are active in repressing furious driving. The cyclist, who suffers from the motor all the time, is, as a.rule, singularly'. silent regarding his grievances, but there can be no doubt that the public are getting more alive to the increasing public dangers pre- sented by the increase of motor traffic, and it is to be hoped that before very long these dangers may be reduced by new legislation for the regulation of motor traffic in the interests of the general public.

INTEREST AT 104 PER CENT. The House of Lords on Tuesday gave judgment in an important money-lending case. A London firm of money-lenders lent to a Mr. Alton, of Derby, since dead, £2,000. The House of Lords held that the claim, which worked out at 104 per cent., was an excessive rate of interest. The money-lenders' appeal against , lower courts' decisions was dismissed with costs.
